# Daily Summary: 2025-05-13

### Visual Instruction Tuning with Chain of Region-of-Interest
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06840v1)
- **Authors**: Yixin Chen, Shuai Zhang, Boran Han, Bernie Wang
- **Abstract**: High-resolution (HR) images are pivotal for enhancing the recognition and understanding capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, directly increasing image resolution can significantly escalate computational demands. In this study, we propose a method called Chain of Region-of-Interest (CoRoI) for Visual Instruction Tuning, aimed at alleviating the computational burden associated with high-resolution images for MLLMs. Drawing inspiration from the selective nature of the human visual system, we recognize that not all regions within high-resolution images carry equal importance. CoRoI seeks to identify and prioritize the most informative regions, thereby enhancing multimodal visual comprehension and recognition while circumventing the need for processing lengthy HR image tokens. Through extensive experiments on 11 benchmarks, we validate the efficacy of CoRoI across varying sizes, ranging from 7B to 34B in parameters. Our models consistently demonstrate superior performance across diverse multimodal benchmarks and tasks. Notably, our method outperforms LLaVA-NeXT on almost all benchmarks and our finetuned 34B model surpasses proprietary methods like Gemini Pro 1.0 on six benchmarks, as well as outperforming GPT-4V on MMB, SEED-I, and MME.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Chain of Region-of-Interest (CoRoI), a visual instruction tuning method designed to enhance the performance of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) with high-resolution images while mitigating computational costs. CoRoI mimics human visual attention by selectively extracting and prioritizing informative regions from high-resolution images based on both visual and language cues (question).  This approach avoids processing the entire high-resolution image and reduces the number of image tokens, injecting the extracted Regions of Interest (RoI) into the LLM's hidden layers through cross-attention mechanisms. The authors conducted extensive experiments across 11 diverse multimodal benchmarks, demonstrating that CoRoI-tuned MLLMs outperform existing methods like LLaVA-NeXT and even proprietary models like Gemini Pro 1.0 and GPT-4V on several tasks.  The paper includes ablation studies, visual examples, and real-world application scenarios to further validate the effectiveness of CoRoI.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The idea of using regions of interest to improve MLLMs isn't entirely new.  Previous works have explored ROI techniques for various vision tasks. However, the specific *chaining* approach, integrating visual and language clues iteratively to guide RoI extraction within an instruction-tuning context, contributes to a novel methodology. The RoI movement and injection into hidden layers are specific design choices that set it apart. The overall integration and the comprehensive experimental validation make this specific implementation of RoI relatively novel.

**Significance:** The significance lies in its potential to improve the efficiency and performance of MLLMs when dealing with high-resolution inputs. The current trend is towards larger and higher-resolution images, which presents a computational bottleneck. CoRoI addresses this by focusing computation on relevant image regions, leading to performance gains with reduced token lengths. The results showing outperformance of strong baselines, including proprietary models, underscore its practical value. Also, It may be suitable in edge and mobile environments.

**Strengths:**

*   **Strong Experimental Validation:** The paper presents a comprehensive set of experiments across a wide range of datasets and tasks, which provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of CoRoI. The direct comparisons with LLaVA-NeXT, Gemini Pro 1.0, and GPT-4V are particularly compelling.
*   **Detailed Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies carefully dissect the contributions of different components of CoRoI, providing insights into the model's inner workings.  This helps to understand the importance of each design decision.
*   **Addresses a Relevant Problem:** Handling high-resolution images efficiently is a crucial challenge for MLLMs.
*   **Visual Examples and Case Studies:**  The visual examples and case studies offer qualitative evidence of CoRoI's ability to focus on relevant image regions and generate accurate responses.
*   Clear writing and modular design make for a better understanding and implementation of the proposed method.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While the RoI chaining idea is relatively novel, it's built upon established concepts of attention and RoI selection.  The core contribution might be viewed as an intelligent integration and adaptation of these concepts, rather than a radical breakthrough.
*   **Dependence on Pre-trained Components:** The model relies on pre-trained ViT and LLM backbones. A truly groundbreaking contribution would involve developing novel vision or language models that intrinsically support high-resolution inputs and RoI processing.
*   **Interpolation Upsampling:** The use of bilinear interpolation to generate high-resolution images could potentially limit the information available to the model. The authors acknowledge this limitation but are constrained by the lack of high-quality high-resolution training data.
*   **Limited Resolution:** Though CoRoI increases the resolution, 1344 × 1344 may still limit its ability to see the benefits that may accrue from extremely-high-resolution images.

**Potential Influence:**

CoRoI can influence future research in several ways:

*   **Stimulate Research on Efficient MLLM Architectures:** The work can inspire further exploration of attention mechanisms, RoI-based processing, and other techniques to improve the efficiency of MLLMs.
*   **Inform the Design of New Benchmarks and Datasets:** The success of CoRoI suggests the need for benchmarks and datasets that specifically evaluate MLLMs' ability to handle high-resolution images and extract relevant information.
*   **Practical Applications:** CoRoI's efficiency makes it well-suited for applications where computational resources are limited, such as mobile devices and edge computing.

**Overall Score:**

Taking into account the strengths and weaknesses, and the relative novelty of the approach, the overall score reflects a significant, but not transformative, contribution.

Score: 7

**Rationale:**  The paper provides a solid and well-validated approach to improving MLLM performance with high-resolution images, addressing a key challenge in the field. The comprehensive experiments and ablation studies strengthen the findings. The limitation of incremental novelty and the dependence on pre-trained components reduce the rating. Although the score is not exceptional, the integration and implementation of existing concepts contribute to an effective and promising solution.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Benign Samples Matter! Fine-tuning On Outlier Benign Samples Severely Breaks Safety
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06843v1)
- **Authors**: Zihan Guan, Mengxuan Hu, Ronghang Zhu, Sheng Li, Anil Vullikanti
- **Abstract**: Recent studies have uncovered a troubling vulnerability in the fine-tuning stage of large language models (LLMs): even fine-tuning on entirely benign datasets can lead to a significant increase in the harmfulness of LLM outputs. Building on this finding, our red teaming study takes this threat one step further by developing a more effective attack. Specifically, we analyze and identify samples within benign datasets that contribute most to safety degradation, then fine-tune LLMs exclusively on these samples. We approach this problem from an outlier detection perspective and propose Self-Inf-N, to detect and extract outliers for fine-tuning. Our findings reveal that fine-tuning LLMs on 100 outlier samples selected by Self-Inf-N in the benign datasets severely compromises LLM safety alignment. Extensive experiments across seven mainstream LLMs demonstrate that our attack exhibits high transferability across different architectures and remains effective in practical scenarios. Alarmingly, our results indicate that most existing mitigation strategies fail to defend against this attack, underscoring the urgent need for more robust alignment safeguards. Codes are available at https://github.com/GuanZihan/Benign-Samples-Matter.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel attack on the safety alignment of large language models (LLMs). It builds upon prior work showing that fine-tuning on benign datasets can degrade safety. The key idea is to strategically select "outlier" samples *within* benign datasets that disproportionately contribute to safety degradation when used for fine-tuning. The authors propose a method called Self-Inf-N, a normalized self-influence score, to identify these outlier samples. Experiments across several LLMs demonstrate that fine-tuning on a small set of outliers selected by Self-Inf-N significantly compromises safety alignment, exhibiting high transferability and persistence even against some mitigation strategies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has moderate novelty. The idea that fine-tuning on seemingly benign data can degrade safety is not entirely new (Qi et al., 2023), nor is the idea of using influence functions for data selection. However, the specific *focus* on identifying and exploiting *outliers within benign datasets* *without* relying on external harmful datasets, and the normalization strategy Self-Inf-N to address length bias in sample selection, represents a step forward. The practical demonstration and systematic evaluation across multiple LLMs, transferability scenarios, and limited mitigation attempts add value. The critical aspect is the refined focus on finding the most harmful samples inside what is expected to be a harmless dataset. This angle of finding "outliers" inside safe distributions is the core of the novelty.
*   **Significance:** The significance is potentially high. The attack is subtle and stealthy, as the outlier samples are not explicitly harmful and might evade common toxicity detection tools. The demonstration of transferability and the difficulty in mitigating the attack raise serious concerns about the robustness of current alignment strategies. The work highlights the need for more sophisticated safeguards that account for the nuanced interplay between data characteristics and model behavior. The results reveal some specific weaknesses with current mitigation strategies. This has important implications to the robustness of alignment pipelines, and should cause developers of LLMs to investigate this vulnerability more.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem and clear motivation.
    *   Systematic experimental evaluation across multiple LLMs and scenarios.
    *   Demonstration of high transferability and persistence.
    *   Analysis of the limitations of existing mitigation strategies.
    *   Clear presentation and well-structured writing.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The attack, while stealthy, still requires fine-tuning, which might raise suspicions in some practical scenarios.
    *   The mitigation attempts explored are limited. A more extensive evaluation of state-of-the-art defenses would strengthen the paper.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth theoretical analysis of why the selected outliers are so effective at degrading safety alignment. Further insight into the specific characteristics of the selected samples (beyond length bias) would enhance the understanding of the vulnerability.
    *   The experiments lack ablations of the different components of Self-Inf-N normalization scheme to demonstrate the contribution of individual steps.
    *   More exploration on varying k in Self-Inf-N extraction would help demonstrate the effect on the harmfulness.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence research in LLM safety and alignment. It provides a valuable case study of a subtle yet powerful attack vector and underscores the importance of robust safeguards. It prompts further investigation into the characteristics of data that can compromise safety alignment and motivates the development of more effective defenses.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the moderate novelty in its core idea, but significant importance in highlighting vulnerabilities, and its systematic experimental evaluation, the paper is scored. While the initial fine-tuning may still raise suspicions, it is significantly less likely to raise red flags compared to fine-tuning using openly malicious datasets. The paper does present a systematic evaluation across multiple LLMs and practical scenarios, and reveals clear weaknesses with existing mitigation strategies, which merits the importance.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Visual Evolutionary Optimization on Combinatorial Problems with Multimodal Large Language Models: A Case Study of Influence Maximization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06850v1)
- **Authors**: Jie Zhao, Kang Hao Cheong
- **Abstract**: Graph-structured combinatorial problems in complex networks are prevalent in many domains, and are computationally demanding due to their complexity and non-linear nature. Traditional evolutionary algorithms (EAs), while robust, often face obstacles due to content-shallow encoding limitations and lack of structural awareness, necessitating hand-crafted modifications for effective application. In this work, we introduce an original framework, Visual Evolutionary Optimization (VEO), leveraging multimodal large language models (MLLMs) as the backbone evolutionary optimizer in this context. Specifically, we propose a context-aware encoding way, representing the solution of the network as an image. In this manner, we can utilize MLLMs' image processing capabilities to intuitively comprehend network configurations, thus enabling machines to solve these problems in a human-like way. We have developed MLLM-based operators tailored for various evolutionary optimization stages, including initialization, crossover, and mutation. Furthermore, we propose that graph sparsification can effectively enhance the applicability and scalability of VEO on large-scale networks, owing to the scale-free nature of real-world networks. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our method using a well-known task in complex networks, influence maximization, and validate it on eight different real-world networks of various structures. The results have confirmed VEO's reliability and enhanced effectiveness compared to traditional evolutionary optimization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Visual Evolutionary Optimization (VEO), a novel framework that leverages multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to solve combinatorial problems on graphs. VEO encodes graph solutions as images, enabling MLLMs to use their image processing capabilities to understand network configurations and perform evolutionary optimization tasks like initialization, crossover, and mutation in a more intuitive, human-like way. The authors also propose graph sparsification techniques to improve the scalability of VEO on large-scale networks.  The framework's effectiveness is demonstrated through a case study on influence maximization, validated on eight real-world networks. The results show that VEO can be more effective than traditional evolutionary algorithms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using MLLMs to guide evolutionary operations by visually representing solutions as images is relatively novel. While prior work has explored LLMs for optimization and some limited applications of MLLMs in graph analysis, this specific combination – using MLLMs as evolutionary operators with a visual encoding tailored for graph-structured problems – is a distinct contribution. The community-level graph sparsification method and its integration with the VEO framework also contribute to the novelty of the approach. The creation of tailored MLLM operators for initialization, crossover and mutation is a clear step.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of VEO is significant. Traditional evolutionary algorithms often struggle with encoding complex structures, limiting their effectiveness in graph-structured problems. By using MLLMs with visual encoding, VEO has the potential to overcome these limitations and achieve superior performance.  The demonstrated improvements in influence maximization, a relevant problem in complex networks, support this potential. The focus on scalability, via graph sparsification, further increases the significance by addressing a key challenge in real-world applications. The work provides interesting insights into how LLMs and evolutionary optimisation can be merged, and might encourage further research in this area.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Original Idea:** The core concept of using MLLMs for visual evolutionary optimization is innovative.
    *   **Context-Aware Operators:** The authors have designed tailored evolutionary operators that use the visual information of graphs.
    *   **Scalability:** The proposed graph sparsification method enhances the scalability of the approach.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The framework is evaluated on diverse real-world networks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependence on MLLMs:** VEO is highly dependent on the capabilities and limitations of the underlying MLLMs, and is sensitive to prompt design. There is an overreliance on the claim that MLLM output mirrors 'human decision making'.
    *   **Generalizability:** While influence maximization serves as a good case study, demonstrating VEO's effectiveness on other graph-structured problems would strengthen the generalizability of the approach.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper doesn't fully address the computational cost associated with using MLLMs in evolutionary optimization, especially considering the API call costs and inference times.
    *   **Limited Baselines:**  The paper mentions comparisons against "traditional evolutionary optimization" but should offer more detail about specific baselines (e.g., what encodings, operators, parameter settings were used?) for the comparisons to be fully convincing.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can stimulate further research in:
    *   MLLM-driven evolutionary optimization.
    *   Visual encoding techniques for graph-structured problems.
    *   Combining graph sparsification with AI-based optimization.
    *   Automated parameter tuning of evolutionary algorithms using LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper demonstrates a novel and promising approach, it has some limitations. The dependence on MLLMs and the need for further validation on diverse problems temper the overall impact. Given these factors, a score of 7 is assigned.

Score: 7

### Benchmarking and Revisiting Code Generation Assessment: A Mutation-Based Approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06880v1)
- **Authors**: Longtian Wang, Tianlin Li, Xiaofei Xie, Yuhan Zhi, Jian Wang, Chao Shen
- **Abstract**: Code Large Language Models (CLLMs) have exhibited outstanding performance in program synthesis, attracting the focus of the research community. The evaluation of CLLM's program synthesis capability has generally relied on manually curated benchmarks. However, there is a substantial gap between real-world scenarios and benchmark settings. Existing benchmarks typically provide only a single input prompt for the evaluation of each synthesis problem. However, in practice, a problem can be described in various ways, including with typos, where developers may struggle to understand certain descriptions and seek clarification to find more suitable wording. Such various descriptions may lead to variations in the performance of CLLMs on the same question, resulting in a biased evaluation when using existing benchmarks. In this paper, we aim to explore these pitfalls with the goal of revisiting and enhancing future benchmark designs. To simulate real-world variations in problem descriptions, we propose 10 mutation strategies and introduce three new metrics to evaluate their impact on code generation. We then assess five popular CLLMs using 12,834 generated prompt variants, and found a significant performance discrepancy between the results from existing benchmarks and those from mutated benchmarks containing perturbations and variations. This finding underscores the need for more robust evaluation methods and benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Benchmarking and Revisiting Code Generation Assessment: A Mutation-Based Approach."

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of current benchmarks used for evaluating code large language models (CLLMs). It argues that these benchmarks, which typically provide a single input prompt per problem, fail to capture the variations and perturbations that exist in real-world scenarios, leading to potentially biased evaluations. To address this, the authors propose a mutation-based approach. They introduce 10 mutation strategies to generate diverse input prompt variants, simulating scenarios like typos, paraphrasing, and variations in the amount of example code provided. They also define three new metrics (Correctness Variability, Mutation Bias, and Best Pass@k) to quantify the impact of these mutations on code generation performance. The authors then evaluate five popular CLLMs using a benchmark augmented with 12,834 generated prompt variants, discovering that the performance significantly varies under different mutation strategies, underscoring the need for more robust evaluation methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using mutation testing techniques on input prompts for evaluating CLLMs is novel and relevant. While mutation testing is a well-established area in software testing, its application to the *input prompts* of code generation models offers a fresh perspective on the evaluation process.  The authors effectively bridge two distinct areas.
*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have significant implications for how CLLMs are evaluated. It convincingly demonstrates that relying solely on existing benchmarks with single prompts can be misleading and that the robustness of CLLMs to input variations is an important, often overlooked, factor. This could lead to fairer and more informative comparative analyses of different CLLMs.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly articulates the problem and motivates the need for a more robust evaluation methodology.
    *   The proposed mutation strategies are well-defined and cover a reasonable range of realistic variations.
    *   The new metrics (CV, MB, Pass@k\_b) provide valuable tools for quantifying the impact of input variations on code generation performance.
    *   The experimental setup is comprehensive, involving a significant number of prompt variants and multiple CLLMs.
    *   The results are well-analyzed and provide actionable insights into the behavior of CLLMs under different input conditions.
    *   The discussion is thoughtful, highlighting the implications of the findings and suggesting directions for future research.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the mutation strategies are reasonable, they might not be exhaustive. Other types of input variations, such as ambiguous or incomplete specifications, could also be considered.
    *   The choice of mutation operations could potentially be further optimized. Some operations might be more relevant or impactful than others, and adaptive mutation strategies could be considered in the future to reflect this.
    *   The study focuses primarily on the HumanEval dataset, which tests functional correctness but does not explicitly address aspects like code style, efficiency, or security. Expanding the evaluation to other datasets with different criteria would further strengthen the findings.
    *   The study's findings are limited by the specific CLLMs investigated. The observed impact of prompt mutations might vary for other models with different architectures or training data.
*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust evaluation frameworks for CLLMs.  It encourages researchers to consider input variations as a key factor in assessing model capabilities and promotes the use of more comprehensive and diverse benchmarks. It highlights a practical issue when evaluating models in realistic usage scenarios. The findings can also inform the development of more robust CLLMs that are less sensitive to input variations and more adaptable to different user formulations of the same problem.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable and novel contribution to the evaluation of code generation models. It identifies a critical gap in existing benchmarks and proposes a practical, well-executed solution to address this gap. While there are some limitations in the scope of mutation strategies and datasets, the paper's findings are compelling and have significant implications for the field. The paper is well-written, clearly articulated, and supported by solid empirical evidence. However, there's room for future work that could explore additional mutation strategies, datasets, or adaptive mutation methods. These improvements would make a greater overall impact on CLLM evaluation.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Image Classification Using a Diffusion Model as a Pre-Training Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06890v1)
- **Authors**: Kosuke Ukita, Ye Xiaolong, Tsuyoshi Okita
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we propose a diffusion model that integrates a representation-conditioning mechanism, where the representations derived from a Vision Transformer (ViT) are used to condition the internal process of a Transformer-based diffusion model. This approach enables representation-conditioned data generation, addressing the challenge of requiring large-scale labeled datasets by leveraging self-supervised learning on unlabeled data. We evaluate our method through a zero-shot classification task for hematoma detection in brain imaging. Compared to the strong contrastive learning baseline, DINOv2, our method achieves a notable improvement of +6.15% in accuracy and +13.60% in F1-score, demonstrating its effectiveness in image classification.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel self-supervised learning framework called Representation-Conditioned Latent Diffusion Transformer for image classification.  It leverages a Transformer-based diffusion model where the denoising process is conditioned on representations derived from a Vision Transformer (ViT). By conditioning on latent representations during the diffusion process, the model aims to capture both global and local features, addressing limitations of contrastive learning approaches.  The method is evaluated on a zero-shot hematoma detection task using brain CT images.  Results demonstrate improved accuracy and F1-score compared to a strong contrastive learning baseline (DINOv2).

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the architecture that integrates ViT-derived representations as conditioning signals within a diffusion model's denoising process. The core concept of using diffusion models for self-supervised representation learning isn't entirely new, but the specific approach of Representation-Conditioned Latent Diffusion Transformer appears to be a novel integration. Specifically, jointly training the ViT encoder and the diffusion model is a notable contribution. Also, explicitly using a diffusion model for pre-training in image classification with this conditioning strategy is new.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant issue in self-supervised learning: the tendency of contrastive methods to focus on global features at the expense of local detail. By using diffusion models, the method attempts to learn more robust and versatile representations. The experimental results, showing improvements over DINOv2 on the hematoma detection task, suggest a promising direction. Medical image analysis is an important application domain.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of contrastive learning and motivates the use of diffusion models.
    * **Novel Architecture:** The Representation-Conditioned Latent Diffusion Transformer represents a novel way to integrate latent representations into the denoising process.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The experiments demonstrate a noticeable improvement over a strong baseline (DINOv2) on a relevant medical imaging task.
    * **Good Ablation Studies:** The paper includes ablation studies by comparing representation-conditioned and unconditional training, which is helpful in determining the impact of the newly proposed element.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The evaluation is primarily limited to one specific medical imaging dataset (brain CT scans for hematoma detection). While the results are encouraging, it's important to assess the generalizability of the method to other datasets and image modalities. It would strengthen the work to include experiments on more general image datasets (e.g., CIFAR-10, ImageNet-derived datasets).
    * **Computational Cost:** Diffusion models are computationally expensive to train.  The paper doesn't thoroughly address the computational cost of the proposed method compared to alternative self-supervised learning approaches.
    * **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The diffusion process is often sensitive to hyperparameters, especially when adapting it to new tasks. The paper mentions the importance of timestep selection but doesn't fully explore the impact of various hyperparameters on performance.
    * **Comparison with Recent Diffusion Classifiers:** Although the paper compares to DINOv2 which is an earlier publication, the paper should also compare with some recent and relevant diffusion-based classification method that utilizes DiT to show the superiority.
* **Potential Impact:**  If the method can be generalized to other datasets and tasks, it could have a significant impact on self-supervised learning for image analysis, particularly in domains where local details are crucial. The ability to effectively leverage unlabeled data while capturing both global and local features is highly valuable.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-defined problem, a novel solution, and promising empirical results.  However, the limited scope of the evaluation and lack of a thorough discussion of computational costs limit its overall impact. Furthermore, although the method is more robust than contrastive learning it is very sensitive to hyperparameters and requires a lot of computational resources, and there is no theoretical guarantee for robustness and efficiency.

### Near-Field Channel Estimation for XL-MIMO: A Deep Generative Model Guided by Side Information
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06900v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenzhou Jin, Li You, Derrick Wing Kwan Ng, Xiang-Gen Xia, Xiqi Gao
- **Abstract**: This paper investigates the near-field (NF) channel estimation (CE) for extremely large-scale multiple-input multiple-output (XL-MIMO) systems. Considering the pronounced NF effects in XL-MIMO communications, we first establish a joint angle-distance (AD) domain-based spherical-wavefront physical channel model that captures the inherent sparsity of XL-MIMO channels. Leveraging the channel's sparsity in the joint AD domain, the CE is approached as a task of reconstructing sparse signals. Anchored in this framework, we first propose a compressed sensing algorithm to acquire a preliminary channel estimate. Harnessing the powerful implicit prior learning capability of generative artificial intelligence (GenAI), we further propose a GenAI-based approach to refine the estimated channel. Specifically, we introduce the preliminary estimated channel as side information, and derive the evidence lower bound (ELBO) of the log-marginal distribution of the target NF channel conditioned on the preliminary estimated channel, which serves as the optimization objective for the proposed generative diffusion model (GDM). Additionally, we introduce a more generalized version of the GDM, the non-Markovian GDM (NM-GDM), to accelerate the sampling process, achieving an approximately tenfold enhancement in sampling efficiency. Experimental results indicate that the proposed approach is capable of offering substantial performance gain in CE compared to existing benchmark schemes within NF XL-MIMO systems. Furthermore, our approach exhibits enhanced generalization capabilities in both the NF or far-field (FF) regions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of channel estimation (CE) in extremely large-scale MIMO (XL-MIMO) systems operating in the near-field (NF) region.  Recognizing the limitations of far-field assumptions in XL-MIMO, it proposes a two-stage approach. The first stage uses a compressed sensing (CS) technique, specifically the Simultaneous Orthogonal Matching Pursuit (SOMP) algorithm, in the joint angle-distance (AD) domain to obtain an initial channel estimate.  The second stage employs a Generative AI (GenAI)-based method, specifically a conditional Generative Diffusion Model (GDM), to refine this initial estimate. The paper introduces a Non-Markovian GDM (NM-GDM) to accelerate the sampling process, improving efficiency. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed NM-GDM approach achieves superior CE performance compared to existing benchmark schemes, even under low SNR and limited communication overhead conditions, while exhibiting good generalization to both NF and FF regions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has reasonable novelty. Combining compressed sensing with generative models for channel estimation is not entirely new, but the specific application to NF XL-MIMO, the use of a joint AD domain representation, and the introduction of a *conditional* GDM *specifically for channel refinement* are novel elements. The NM-GDM for accelerated sampling is also a positive contribution. However, conditional generative models are an established approach in machine learning, so the adaptation itself is not a groundbreaking innovation.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant and important problem. Accurate CE is crucial for the successful deployment of XL-MIMO, and the NF effects become significant with extremely large arrays. Therefore, improved CE techniques tailored to this scenario are highly desirable. The results showing performance gains and robustness under various conditions suggest a practical improvement over existing methods. The efficiency gains offered by the NM-GDM contribute to the practical viability of the proposed CE technique. However, the paper lacks a detailed computational complexity analysis, which limits the practical significance score.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The joint AD domain modeling accurately reflects the physics of NF channels.
    *   The use of a conditional GDM allows for leveraging prior channel information to refine the CS-based initial estimate.
    *   The NM-GDM accelerates the sampling process, addressing a key limitation of diffusion models.
    *   The experimental results are comprehensive, comparing against several baselines and evaluating performance under different conditions (SNR, antenna count, pilot length, NF/FF). The ablation study provides insights into design choices.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The computational complexity of the GDM, even with NM-GDM, could be a concern for real-time implementations. A more detailed analysis would strengthen the paper. The paper states that the approach exploits fully convolutional nature, however it does not present the computational cost of the fully convolutional operation.
    *   While the paper compares with several CS-based methods, it would be beneficial to also compare against the latest (and more performant) deep-learning based channel estimators to ensure the superiority of NM-GDM channel estimator.
    *   The improvement in performance vs state-of-the-art does not present particularly significant performance gains. The genie-aided bound also serves to emphasize the limits of the proposed method.

*   **Potential Impact:** If the proposed NM-GDM channel refinement approach proves to be practically viable, it could influence the design of CE algorithms for future XL-MIMO systems. The emphasis on learned implicit priors and accelerated sampling could inspire further research in this direction.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a technically sound and relevant contribution to the field of XL-MIMO channel estimation. The use of a conditional GDM and NM-GDM demonstrates reasonable performance improvement. However, some limitations exist that impact the practical significance of the approach.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: eess.SP
- **Score**: 7/10

### Ecco: Improving Memory Bandwidth and Capacity for LLMs via Entropy-aware Cache Compression
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06901v1)
- **Authors**: Feng Cheng, Cong Guo, Chiyue Wei, Junyao Zhang, Changchun Zhou, Edward Hanson, Jiaqi Zhang, Xiaoxiao Liu, Hai "Helen" Li, Yiran Chen
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated transformative capabilities across diverse artificial intelligence applications, yet their deployment is hindered by substantial memory and computational demands, especially in resource-constrained environments. Quantization techniques have emerged as a critical solution, reducing data precision to enhance memory and computational efficiency. However, existing methods often suffer from high runtime overheads and potential accuracy degradation. To address these challenges, we propose Ecco, an entropy-based cache compression technique tailored for LLMs. Ecco combines group-wise and non-uniform quantization with pre-defined shared k-means patterns and Huffman coding to exploit the inherent entropy characteristics of LLM cache data. Recognizing the inefficiencies of traditional Huffman coding in terms of parallelism and latency, we introduce a novel parallel Huffman-based decoding process with a multi-stage pipeline design, reducing latency by two orders of magnitude and achieving throughput comparable to GPU L2 caches. Comprehensive evaluations demonstrate that Ecco achieves an up to 2.9$\times$ and 1.9$\times$ speedup over the state-of-the-art AWQ and SmoothQuant framework, 2.4$\times$ over the Olive accelerator, all while increasing memory capacity by nearly 4$\times$ and maintaining state-of-the-art LLM accuracy. These results underscore the effectiveness of our entropy-based cache compression in enhancing LLM performance and efficiency, paving the way for more deployable large-scale AI models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Ecco: Improving Memory Bandwidth and Capacity for LLMs via Entropy-aware Cache Compression" introduces Ecco, a novel cache compression technique tailored for Large Language Models (LLMs). Ecco combines group-wise, non-uniform quantization with shared k-means patterns and Huffman coding to exploit the inherent entropy characteristics of LLM cache data. To address the limitations of traditional Huffman coding, the paper proposes a parallel Huffman-based decoding process with a multi-stage pipeline.  The evaluation demonstrates speedups over state-of-the-art quantization frameworks like AWQ and Olive, along with increased memory capacity and maintained LLM accuracy. The key claim is that Ecco effectively reduces memory bandwidth demands and enhances LLM performance and efficiency, making LLMs more deployable.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The combination of techniques presented in Ecco, while not entirely new individually, represents a *novel integration* within the context of LLM cache compression. The key novelty lies in the parallel Huffman decoding pipeline, which aims to address the parallelism and latency limitations of traditional Huffman coding in hardware. The shared k-means pattern quantization approach also presents a useful combination of existing techniques in a new context. Prior work has explored various compression techniques for LLMs, and this work makes a specific contribution to hardware acceleration through cache compression.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its potential to address a crucial bottleneck in LLM deployment: memory bandwidth and capacity. The experimental results demonstrating speedups and memory capacity gains are promising and suggest that Ecco could be a valuable technique for improving the efficiency of LLM inference, especially in resource-constrained environments. However, the actual real-world impact would depend on the ease of integration into existing hardware architectures and the overhead of the compression/decompression circuitry. If these can be adequately managed, the potential impact is substantial.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly identifies the problem of memory bandwidth limitations in LLMs.
    *   The proposed Ecco architecture offers a comprehensive solution by combining multiple compression techniques.
    *   The parallel Huffman decoding pipeline is a significant contribution.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate tangible performance improvements over existing methods.
    *   The detailed hardware area and power estimation adds to the credibility of the study.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper relies heavily on simulation results and lacks a real hardware implementation. The performance reported may be optimistic if the effects of memory contention, cache coherence, and other system-level effects are not accurately modelled.
    *   The paper could benefit from a deeper discussion about the complexity and area requirements for implementing the proposed parallel Huffman decoder. Although area overhead is evaluated, the scaling behavior with respect to cache size is not sufficiently explained.
    *   The analysis should provide more insights on how performance varies for differing architectural designs (e.g., different levels of parallelization, cache sizes, and memory bandwidths.)
    *   The paper makes limited discussion around energy efficiency. The authors should include details on how it improves energy efficency.
    *   The paper offers limited ablative analysis. For instance, the actual gain attained by padding the small Huffman sequence cannot be determined.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper could influence research on hardware acceleration for LLMs by highlighting the potential of cache compression techniques. It could also inspire further research into more efficient hardware implementations of entropy coding and other compression algorithms. It could be influential to hardware designers working on LLM accelerators.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper demonstrates a tangible improvement in LLM performance and memory efficiency using a novel cache compression technique. However, the lack of a real hardware implementation and a more comprehensive system-level analysis limits the overall impact of the study. While the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field, more work is needed to validate the simulation results and demonstrate the practicality of the proposed architecture. Therefore, a score of 7 reflects a solid, but not exceptional, contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.AR
- **Score**: 7/10

### EcoLANG: Efficient and Effective Agent Communication Language Induction for Social Simulation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06904v1)
- **Authors**: Xinyi Mou, Chen Qian, Wei Liu, Xuanjing Huang, Zhongyu Wei
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated an impressive ability to role-play humans and replicate complex social dynamics. While large-scale social simulations are gaining increasing attention, they still face significant challenges, particularly regarding high time and computation costs. Existing solutions, such as distributed mechanisms or hybrid agent-based model (ABM) integrations, either fail to address inference costs or compromise accuracy and generalizability. To this end, we propose EcoLANG: Efficient and Effective Agent Communication Language Induction for Social Simulation. EcoLANG operates in two stages: (1) language evolution, where we filter synonymous words and optimize sentence-level rules through natural selection, and (2) language utilization, where agents in social simulations communicate using the evolved language. Experimental results demonstrate that EcoLANG reduces token consumption by over 20%, enhancing efficiency without sacrificing simulation accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary, critical evaluation, and novelty/significance score for the paper "EcoLANG: Efficient and Effective Agent Communication Language Induction for Social Simulation":

**Summary:**

The paper "EcoLANG" addresses the high computational cost of large-scale social simulations driven by Large Language Models (LLMs).  It proposes a two-stage framework to induce a more efficient communication language for agents in these simulations. The first stage, *language evolution*, involves vocabulary compression through synonym filtering and rule-based optimization using natural selection.  The second stage, *language utilization*, integrates the evolved language into the LLM-driven agents.  Experiments on diverse social simulation tasks demonstrate that EcoLANG significantly reduces token consumption while preserving simulation accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper tackles a significant practical problem in LLM-driven social simulations - high computational cost.
    *   The EcoLANG framework is well-defined and conceptually clear, combining vocabulary reduction with rule evolution in a compelling way.
    *   The experiments are reasonably comprehensive, covering several social simulation scenarios and comparing against relevant baselines.  The results convincingly demonstrate the token consumption reduction of EcoLANG.
    *   The paper acknowledges limitations and discusses potential future directions.
    * Transferability to multiple LLMs is demonstrated by testing on Llama, Mistral and Qwen.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The improvements demonstrated, while significant (20% token reduction), might not be transformative enough to make a qualitative difference in large-scale simulation feasibility. It relies on token reduction and does not address the reduction of the number of inference times.
    *   The evaluation metrics rely heavily on LLM-based judging which might have their biases.  The comparison with real-world communication is limited to relatively simple metrics like token counts and similarity scores.
    * The approach transfers evolution to other LLMs, but requires the evolution itself to be performed in a specific model.
    *   The paper doesn't deeply address the potential for EcoLANG to alter the emergent dynamics within the simulations. While accuracy is maintained, it's unclear if the specific *pathways* to those outcomes are affected by the simplified language.
    *   Ethical considerations, while mentioned, could be explored in greater depth, particularly regarding the impact of simplified communication on the representativeness of the simulation.
    * Agent Torch with prototype creation might introduce a strong bias because some important voices might be lost.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The idea of *inducing* a communication language specifically tailored for efficient social simulation within an LLM framework is relatively novel.  While other approaches focus on distributed systems, ABM or structured languages, EcoLANG offers a distinct approach by focusing on the language itself. The method is also relatively task-agnostic. The significance stems from its potential to unlock more extensive and realistic social simulations, leading to improved insights in social science.

**Justification for Score:**

EcoLANG presents a valuable contribution to the field by addressing a practical challenge in LLM-driven social simulations and introducing a novel approach to reducing computational costs. The paper is well-structured, provides solid empirical evidence, and acknowledges its limitations. The proposed method is of moderate impact.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Artificial General or Personalized Intelligence? A Survey on Foundation Models for Personalized Federated Intelligence
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06907v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Qiao, Huy Q. Le, Avi Deb Raha, Phuong-Nam Tran, Apurba Adhikary, Mengchun Zhang, Loc X. Nguyen, Eui-Nam Huh, Dusit Niyato, Choong Seon Hong
- **Abstract**: The rise of large language models (LLMs), such as ChatGPT, DeepSeek, and Grok-3, has reshaped the artificial intelligence landscape. As prominent examples of foundational models (FMs) built on LLMs, these models exhibit remarkable capabilities in generating human-like content, bringing us closer to achieving artificial general intelligence (AGI). However, their large-scale nature, sensitivity to privacy concerns, and substantial computational demands present significant challenges to personalized customization for end users. To bridge this gap, this paper presents the vision of artificial personalized intelligence (API), focusing on adapting these powerful models to meet the specific needs and preferences of users while maintaining privacy and efficiency. Specifically, this paper proposes personalized federated intelligence (PFI), which integrates the privacy-preserving advantages of federated learning (FL) with the zero-shot generalization capabilities of FMs, enabling personalized, efficient, and privacy-protective deployment at the edge. We first review recent advances in both FL and FMs, and discuss the potential of leveraging FMs to enhance federated systems. We then present the key motivations behind realizing PFI and explore promising opportunities in this space, including efficient PFI, trustworthy PFI, and PFI empowered by retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). Finally, we outline key challenges and future research directions for deploying FM-powered FL systems at the edge with improved personalization, computational efficiency, and privacy guarantees. Overall, this survey aims to lay the groundwork for the development of API as a complement to AGI, with a particular focus on PFI as a key enabling technique.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a survey on the intersection of Foundation Models (FMs) and Federated Learning (FL), proposing a vision for "Artificial Personalized Intelligence" (API). It identifies the limitations of solely pursuing Artificial General Intelligence (AGI) due to privacy concerns, computational demands, and the need for personalized customization. The authors introduce Personalized Federated Intelligence (PFI) as a key enabling technique. PFI integrates the privacy-preserving advantages of FL with the zero-shot generalization capabilities of FMs, enabling personalized, efficient, and privacy-protective deployment at the edge. The survey reviews recent advances in both FL and FMs, discusses the potential of leveraging FMs to enhance federated systems, explores promising opportunities, including efficient, trustworthy, and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG)-empowered PFI. It outlines key challenges (heterogeneity, communication overhead, privacy, personalization vs. generalization) and future research directions (Meta-PFI, quantum-enabled PFI, sustainable intelligence), emphasizing improved personalization, computational efficiency, and privacy guarantees.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The paper's core contribution lies in its articulation of API and PFI as a paradigm shift, moving beyond solely pursuing AGI towards user-centric and privacy-conscious AI development.  While surveys on FL and FMs exist, the explicit focus on *personalized* FL leveraging FMs is less explored, making this a valuable contribution. The detailed exploration of RAG-enhanced PFI, along with the introduction of concepts like Meta-PFI and quantum-enabled PFI, adds originality. However, much of the content surveys existing work. The real novelty is in the proposed vision and the framework presented, and while promising, they are still at a conceptual stage.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Comprehensive overview of FL and FMs, including a useful taxonomy of FMs.
    *   Clear articulation of the limitations of current AGI-centric approaches.
    *   Well-structured organization and clear writing style, making complex concepts accessible.
    *   The detailed discussion of challenges and potential opportunities, including RAG, contributes significantly to understanding the scope of future research.
    *   The inclusion of future directions (Meta-PFI, quantum-enabled, sustainable) demonstrates forward-thinking vision.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The PFI framework is mostly conceptual and lacks empirical validation or concrete case studies. While future directions are discussed, the practicality and feasibility of concepts like quantum-enabled PFI require further substantiation.
    *   The mitigation strategies against threats in PFI are primarily a survey of existing techniques rather than new solutions tailored to the FM + FL context. The critical evaluation of those mitigation strategies given this specific context is somewhat limited.
    *   Some sections heavily rely on existing literature and could benefit from a more in-depth analysis and synthesis.
    *   While the paper mentions existing surveys in this area, the comparison in Table I is too broad and doesn't capture the nuances of related survey work (e.g. how it relates to the specific task of *personalization* of federated FMs, which is the core focus of the current paper).
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the research direction in federated learning and foundation models. It can inspire researchers to prioritize personalization, privacy, and efficiency in the design of future AI systems. The emphasis on RAG and the proposed future directions offer a concrete roadmap for further investigation.
*   **Justification:** The paper takes a broad view of AI development, clearly lays out the challenges of current approaches, and provides a useful framework for future research. It is well-written and well-organized. However, the lack of empirical validation and heavy reliance on existing literature temper the novelty and immediate impact. The survey is well-structured and insightful, it provides a roadmap for future research at the intersection of federated learning and foundation models, but requires concrete implementation and testing. The proposed vision is intriguing, the current instantiation is primarily conceptual in scope.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Building a Human-Verified Clinical Reasoning Dataset via a Human LLM Hybrid Pipeline for Trustworthy Medical AI
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06912v1)
- **Authors**: Chao Ding, Mouxiao Bian, Pengcheng Chen, Hongliang Zhang, Tianbin Li, Lihao Liu, Jiayuan Chen, Zhuoran Li, Yabei Zhong, Yongqi Liu, Haiqing Huang, Dongming Shan, Junjun He, Jie Xu
- **Abstract**: Despite strong performance in medical question-answering, the clinical adoption of Large Language Models (LLMs) is critically hampered by their opaque 'black-box' reasoning, limiting clinician trust. This challenge is compounded by the predominant reliance of current medical LLMs on corpora from scientific literature or synthetic data, which often lack the granular expert validation and high clinical relevance essential for advancing their specialized medical capabilities. To address these critical gaps, we introduce a highly clinically relevant dataset with 31,247 medical question-answer pairs, each accompanied by expert-validated chain-of-thought (CoT) explanations. This resource, spanning multiple clinical domains, was curated via a scalable human-LLM hybrid pipeline: LLM-generated rationales were iteratively reviewed, scored, and refined by medical experts against a structured rubric, with substandard outputs revised through human effort or guided LLM regeneration until expert consensus. This publicly available dataset provides a vital source for the development of medical LLMs that capable of transparent and verifiable reasoning, thereby advancing safer and more interpretable AI in medicine.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new, large-scale Chinese medical QA dataset called MedCoT, consisting of 30,000 expert-validated question-answer pairs with chain-of-thought explanations. The dataset was created using a human-LLM hybrid pipeline: LLMs (specifically DeepSeek-R1) generated initial QA pairs and explanations, which were then iteratively reviewed, refined, and scored by medical experts against a structured rubric.  The rubric assessed medical correctness, reasoning structure, information sufficiency, terminology clarity, and clinical utility.  A five-strike system was implemented, where questions that consistently stumped the LLM were escalated to an expert panel for deeper review. The authors demonstrate the dataset's utility by fine-tuning models on it and showing improved performance compared to models trained on unvalidated data. The dataset is publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its *methodological approach* to dataset creation and the resulting *high-quality, expert-validated medical reasoning dataset* in Chinese. While other medical QA datasets exist (mentioned in the paper like MedQA, MedReason and Huatuo-o1 COT), this work emphasizes deep, iterative human involvement throughout the data generation and validation process, addressing a crucial gap in existing resources. This expert-centric approach is the differentiating factor. They are going further than simply validating data.

**Significance:**  The paper addresses a critical challenge in the adoption of LLMs in medicine: the lack of trust due to the "black-box" nature of reasoning. A dataset with transparent, verifiable, and clinically relevant CoT explanations contributes significantly to building more trustworthy medical AI.  It enables the development of models capable of not just answering questions but also justifying their answers in a manner understandable and acceptable to clinicians. This could potentially improve patient safety and clinical decision-making. The public availability of the dataset is also a significant contribution, enabling further research and development in this area. The empirical validation showing improved model performance on their dataset provides a solid evidence of it's usefulness.

**Strengths:**

*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The human-LLM hybrid approach, with iterative expert review and a structured rubric, is a significant strength. The five-strike system is innovative for identifying challenging questions.
*   **Clinically Relevant Content:** Focus on real-world clinical scenarios and expert validation ensures that the dataset is not just factually correct but also clinically meaningful.
*   **Multi-Dimensional Evaluation:** The comprehensive rubric provides a granular view of CoT quality, enabling targeted improvements in model training.
*   **Public Availability:** Making the dataset publicly available promotes reproducibility and fosters further research.
*   **Well written:** The paper does a good job in contrasting their approach with existing datasets.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Language and Regional Focus:** The dataset is in Chinese and focused on Chinese medical scenarios. While the methodology is transferable, the dataset's direct applicability may be limited to certain regions and languages.
*   **Model Bias:** The initial data generation relied solely on DeepSeek-R1.  The authors acknowledge that this may introduce model-specific biases.
*   **Resource Intensive:** Expert validation is resource-intensive, which could limit the dataset's scalability.
*   **Single Choice Format:** The single choice QA format, while common, might not fully capture the complexities of medical reasoning in some cases.

**Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the development of more trustworthy and explainable AI in healthcare, particularly in the Chinese medical context. The methodology could be adapted to create similar datasets in other languages and medical domains. It can potentially guide future research on human-AI collaboration for data curation.

**Score:** 8

**Rationale:**  The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of medical AI by addressing the critical need for trustworthy reasoning in LLMs. The rigorous methodology, expert validation, and public availability make this dataset a valuable resource for researchers and developers. However, the Chinese language focus, potential model bias, and resource-intensive validation process are limitations that prevent it from achieving a higher score.  While not a groundbreaking theoretical breakthrough, the practical value and the careful execution of the data curation process makes it a significant step forward and worth the "8".

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Unsupervised Learning for Class Distribution Mismatch
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06948v1)
- **Authors**: Pan Du, Wangbo Zhao, Xinai Lu, Nian Liu, Zhikai Li, Chaoyu Gong, Suyun Zhao, Hong Chen, Cuiping Li, Kai Wang, Yang You
- **Abstract**: Class distribution mismatch (CDM) refers to the discrepancy between class distributions in training data and target tasks. Previous methods address this by designing classifiers to categorize classes known during training, while grouping unknown or new classes into an "other" category. However, they focus on semi-supervised scenarios and heavily rely on labeled data, limiting their applicability and performance. To address this, we propose Unsupervised Learning for Class Distribution Mismatch (UCDM), which constructs positive-negative pairs from unlabeled data for classifier training. Our approach randomly samples images and uses a diffusion model to add or erase semantic classes, synthesizing diverse training pairs. Additionally, we introduce a confidence-based labeling mechanism that iteratively assigns pseudo-labels to valuable real-world data and incorporates them into the training process. Extensive experiments on three datasets demonstrate UCDM's superiority over previous semi-supervised methods. Specifically, with a 60% mismatch proportion on Tiny-ImageNet dataset, our approach, without relying on labeled data, surpasses OpenMatch (with 40 labels per class) by 35.1%, 63.7%, and 72.5% in classifying known, unknown, and new classes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel unsupervised learning approach, UCDM (Unsupervised Learning for Class Distribution Mismatch), to address the class distribution mismatch (CDM) problem. Unlike existing semi-supervised methods that rely on labeled data, UCDM constructs positive-negative pairs from unlabeled data using a diffusion model. The diffusion model is used to add or erase semantic classes, creating diverse training pairs.  A confidence-based labeling mechanism iteratively assigns pseudo-labels to real-world data and incorporates them into the training process. Experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and Tiny-ImageNet demonstrate that UCDM outperforms previous semi-supervised methods, particularly in scenarios with high mismatch proportions and without reliance on any labeled data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main contribution lies in proposing a *fully unsupervised* method for tackling the CDM problem. Shifting away from the semi-supervised setting, especially in the context of CDM, is significant. The creative use of diffusion models to synthesize training data by adding and erasing semantic classes is a good innovation. Conditional DDIM inversion for generating negative samples, although conceptually related to existing techniques, is adapted specifically to this CDM scenario. The confidence-based labeling is standard, but it's the *context* of its application (unsupervised, diffusion-generated data) that adds value. The two theorems help support the paper by giving some mathmatical support for what it claims it does with the use of diffusion models.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to relax the requirement for labeled data in CDM. This has practical implications in scenarios where labels are scarce or expensive to obtain. The reported performance improvements over semi-supervised methods (particularly OpenMatch), *without using labels*, are compelling. Demonstrating strong performance in both closed-set and open-set tasks further strengthens the contribution. The focus on the balance score and the attempt to address the challenge that is a highly imbalance dataset makes it an attempt to tackle something new.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The unsupervised nature of the approach is a key advantage.
    *   The effective utilization of diffusion models for data synthesis is well-motivated.
    *   The experimental results are convincing, demonstrating superior performance compared to semi-supervised baselines *without* labels.
    *   The paper is well-written and structured, making it relatively easy to understand the proposed method and its rationale.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on diffusion models may introduce biases inherent in these models, which might affect the generalization performance. The method may be limited by the quality of the pre-trained diffusion model that is used.
    *   The improved results are not consistently as large as they could be for it to be seen as a high impact change.
    *   While the confidence-based labeling helps, it may still introduce noisy labels, potentially limiting the upper bound of performance. While the paper is structured well, it is at times difficult to follow.

*   **Impact:** If the method proves robust and generalizable across various datasets and tasks, it could encourage further research into unsupervised CDM approaches. The techniques of training without ground truth data is a fairly valuable innovation to the field. The idea of using synthetic images, although not completely novel, is very effective within this UCDM method. Other works can use this to expand the training data for other tasks.

*   **Score:** 6.  While the core idea is significant and opens a new direction, several aspects limit the potential impact. The reliance on diffusion models may present practical challenges in terms of computational cost and potential biases. The innovation of only requiring ground truth class names but not ground truth data for creating the synthetic training data is nice. Given these factors, and considering potential limitations, a score of 6 reflects a solid contribution with potential for future development, but not a paradigm-shifting breakthrough.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### From Knowledge to Reasoning: Evaluating LLMs for Ionic Liquids Research in Chemical and Biological Engineering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06964v1)
- **Authors**: Gaurab Sarkar, Sougata Saha
- **Abstract**: Although Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable performance in diverse general knowledge and reasoning tasks, their utility in the scientific domain of Chemical and Biological Engineering (CBE) is unclear. Hence, it necessitates challenging evaluation benchmarks that can measure LLM performance in knowledge- and reasoning-based tasks, which is lacking. As a foundational step, we empirically measure the reasoning capabilities of LLMs in CBE. We construct and share an expert-curated dataset of 5,920 examples for benchmarking LLMs' reasoning capabilities in the niche domain of Ionic Liquids (ILs) for carbon sequestration, an emergent solution to reducing global warming. The dataset presents different difficulty levels by varying along the dimensions of linguistic and domain-specific knowledge. Benchmarking three less than 10B parameter open-source LLMs on the dataset suggests that while smaller general-purpose LLMs are knowledgeable about ILs, they lack domain-specific reasoning capabilities. Based on our results, we further discuss considerations for leveraging LLMs for carbon capture research using ILs. Since LLMs have a high carbon footprint, gearing them for IL research can symbiotically benefit both fields and help reach the ambitious carbon neutrality target by 2050. Dataset link: https://github.com/sougata-ub/llms_for_ionic_liquids
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "From Knowledge to Reasoning: Evaluating LLMs for Ionic Liquids Research in Chemical and Biological Engineering":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the lack of specific benchmarks for evaluating the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in the Chemical and Biological Engineering (CBE) domain, particularly in the area of Ionic Liquids (ILs) for carbon capture. The authors construct an expert-curated dataset of 5,920 examples designed to benchmark LLMs' reasoning capabilities related to ILs. The dataset varies in difficulty along linguistic and domain-specific knowledge dimensions.  They benchmark three open-source LLMs (Llama 3.1-7B, Mistral-7B, and Gemma-9B) and analyze their factual knowledge and reasoning abilities. The results indicate that while the models possess general knowledge about ILs, they struggle with domain-specific reasoning. The paper concludes by discussing the implications of these findings for leveraging LLMs in carbon capture research and suggests directions for future work, including fine-tuning and the potential for LLMs to contribute to carbon neutrality by improving IL research efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty of the paper lies in its creation of a domain-specific benchmark for evaluating LLMs in CBE. While LLMs have been used in material science and protein engineering, this is a relatively unexplored intersection, especially the focus on a niche application like ionic liquids for carbon capture.  The design of the entailment task and the systematic variation of difficulty factors (incorrectness levels, paraphrasing, number of options) represent a thoughtful approach to assessing different aspects of LLM reasoning.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant because it highlights the limitations of general-purpose LLMs in specialized scientific domains. It makes a strong case for the need for domain-specific benchmarks and fine-tuning to effectively utilize LLMs in areas like CBE. The discussion of how LLMs can be used to accelerate IL research and potentially reduce their own carbon footprint (by making carbon capture more efficient) is also noteworthy.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Dataset Creation:** The meticulous process of dataset creation, involving expert annotation and a detailed methodology for generating incorrect and paraphrased propositions, is a major strength.  The inclusion of different difficulty levels adds to the dataset's value.
    *   **Experimental Design:** The experimental setup is well-defined, with clearly articulated hypotheses and a systematic evaluation of the models across different conditions. The use of multiple LLMs and the comparison of their performance provide a solid basis for the conclusions.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and organized, with clear explanations of the methodology, results, and implications.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Model Limitations:** The evaluation is limited to three relatively small, open-source models (less than 10B parameters). While the authors acknowledge this limitation, it does raise questions about the generalizability of the findings to larger, more powerful models. Experiments with larger, and potentially closed-API, models should be included.
    *   **Limited Reasoning Tasks:** The study focuses primarily on an entailment task. While this is a useful starting point, it could be beneficial to incorporate other reasoning tasks, such as question answering or multi-hop inference, to provide a more comprehensive assessment of LLM capabilities. It would be interesting to examine how LLMs handle counterfactual situations or simulate experimental results.
    *   **Fine-tuning:** The paper does not explore the effects of fine-tuning LLMs on the dataset. Fine-tuning could significantly improve performance on the domain-specific reasoning tasks and shed light on the potential for adapting LLMs to CBE applications.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the development of more targeted evaluation benchmarks and fine-tuning strategies for LLMs in specialized scientific domains. It may also encourage further research into the potential applications of LLMs in areas like materials discovery and carbon capture.
    *   The paper does not delve into what types of ionic liquids are most promising or focus experiments in these areas. It may be helpful to focus LLMs to find more information in that regard to increase effectiveness.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the paper's novelty in creating a specific benchmark for LLMs in CBE, its clear methodology, and its valuable insights into the limitations of general-purpose LLMs in this field, but also considering its limitations regarding experiment complexity, size of models used and lack of fine-tuning the models, I assign a score of:

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### High-Frequency Prior-Driven Adaptive Masking for Accelerating Image Super-Resolution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06975v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Shang, Dongwei Ren, Wanying Zhang, Pengfei Zhu, Qinghua Hu, Wangmeng Zuo
- **Abstract**: The primary challenge in accelerating image super-resolution lies in reducing computation while maintaining performance and adaptability. Motivated by the observation that high-frequency regions (e.g., edges and textures) are most critical for reconstruction, we propose a training-free adaptive masking module for acceleration that dynamically focuses computation on these challenging areas. Specifically, our method first extracts high-frequency components via Gaussian blur subtraction and adaptively generates binary masks using K-means clustering to identify regions requiring intensive processing. Our method can be easily integrated with both CNNs and Transformers. For CNN-based architectures, we replace standard $3 \times 3$ convolutions with an unfold operation followed by $1 \times 1$ convolutions, enabling pixel-wise sparse computation guided by the mask. For Transformer-based models, we partition the mask into non-overlapping windows and selectively process tokens based on their average values. During inference, unnecessary pixels or windows are pruned, significantly reducing computation. Moreover, our method supports dilation-based mask adjustment to control the processing scope without retraining, and is robust to unseen degradations (e.g., noise, compression). Extensive experiments on benchmarks demonstrate that our method reduces FLOPs by 24--43% for state-of-the-art models (e.g., CARN, SwinIR) while achieving comparable or better quantitative metrics. The source code is available at https://github.com/shangwei5/AMSR
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a training-free adaptive masking module to accelerate image super-resolution (SR). The core idea is to dynamically allocate computational resources by focusing on high-frequency regions (edges, textures) which are deemed most critical for SR reconstruction. The method extracts these high-frequency components via Gaussian blur subtraction and adaptively generates binary masks using K-means clustering.  This mask is then used to guide computation by pruning unnecessary pixels/tokens during inference. For CNNs, standard convolutions are replaced by an unfold operation followed by 1x1 convolutions to enable pixel-wise sparse computation. For Transformers, masks are partitioned into windows, and tokens are selectively processed based on their average values.  The method supports dilation-based mask adjustments without retraining to handle variations in degradations. The authors demonstrate FLOPs reduction for state-of-the-art SR models while maintaining or improving quantitative metrics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its combination of existing techniques in a specific application with a focus on training-free acceleration. While the individual components (Gaussian blur for frequency extraction, K-means for clustering, unfold operation for CNNs, token pruning for Transformers) are not novel in themselves, their integration into a cohesive, training-free masking module for SR acceleration is a reasonable contribution. The claim of "architecture-agnostic computation pruning" is partially valid, but the implementations are significantly different for CNNs vs. Transformers, limiting the true "agnostic" nature. The most significant novelty is the *training-free* aspect. Existing methods generally rely on training a classifier/regressor to determine region complexity and hence compute allocation. Removing this reliance simplifies the pipeline and reduces complexity.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of the work is primarily in its practical impact. Accelerating SR models is a crucial goal for real-world applications, and the paper demonstrates a tangible improvement in efficiency (FLOPs reduction) with minimal or no performance degradation. The training-free aspect also increases the applicability of the method, as it doesn't require re-training the masking module for new datasets or architectures. The reported results indicate substantial performance gains for many architectures (CARN, SwinIR). Further demonstrating the computational benefits on actual hardware and benchmarking with other acceleration techniques would strengthen the significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Training-free nature is a significant advantage.
    *   Clear and well-explained methodology.
