    *   Demonstrated FLOPs reduction on state-of-the-art models.
    *   Good experimental validation on benchmark datasets.
    *   Adaptation to CNNs and Transformers, increasing the scope.
    *   Robustness to unseen degradation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The novelty of individual components is limited.
    *   The architecture agnosticism is overstated; the CNN and Transformer implementations are quite distinct.
    *   The effectiveness of this approach is closely linked to the underlying SR architecture's body, exhibiting less pronounced performance in models with smaller bodies.
    *   Lack of rigorous hardware benchmarking against existing acceleration techniques.
    *   The adaptive K-means clustering is simple, could be explored more advanced methods.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by promoting training-free acceleration strategies for SR. The work could inspire further research into developing more efficient and adaptable masking techniques and exploring combinations with other acceleration methods. The paper can inspire additional research into architecture-specific adaptation.

*   **Overall Assessment:** The paper offers a practical and valuable contribution to the field of SR acceleration. The training-free approach is a compelling advantage. While the individual components are not revolutionary, their integration and the demonstrated performance gains justify a positive evaluation. While it does have limitations, the ease of implementation, reduced complexity and comparable performance makes this work worth considering.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**
The score reflects a solid contribution but stops short of exceptional due to the lack of groundbreaking novelty.  The practical impact and well-demonstrated performance make it a worthwhile addition to the literature, but the incremental nature of the improvements limits its transformative potential. It could benefit from more innovative steps like more advanced K-means, an analysis between the different levels of complexities (e.g. the thresholding strategy) or a more architecture-agnostic approach.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Convert Language Model into a Value-based Strategic Planner
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06987v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoyu Wang, Yue Zhao, Qingqing Gu, Zhonglin Jiang, Xiaokai Chen, Yong Chen, Luo Ji
- **Abstract**: Emotional support conversation (ESC) aims to alleviate the emotional distress of individuals through effective conversations. Although large language models (LLMs) have obtained remarkable progress on ESC, most of these studies might not define the diagram from the state model perspective, therefore providing a suboptimal solution for long-term satisfaction. To address such an issue, we leverage the Q-learning on LLMs, and propose a framework called straQ*. Our framework allows a plug-and-play LLM to bootstrap the planning during ESC, determine the optimal strategy based on long-term returns, and finally guide the LLM to response. Substantial experiments on ESC datasets suggest that straQ* outperforms many baselines, including direct inference, self-refine, chain of thought, finetuning, and finite state machines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces straQ*, a framework that converts a Language Model (LLM) into a value-based strategic planner for emotional support conversations (ESC). The approach leverages Q-learning on LLMs to determine optimal strategies based on long-term returns, guiding the LLM to generate more effective and supportive responses. The framework uses the Deep Q-learning (DQN) algorithm on LLMs to provide a strategic Q function, with the strategy as a textual action.  The paper evaluates the framework on ESC datasets and demonstrates that straQ* outperforms various baselines.  The authors explore two reward mechanisms, imitation and distillation, with distillation showing better performance in human evaluation and generalization. The key contributions are the formulation of ESC as a strategy-level MDP, the finetuning of pretrained LLMs via the Bellman Equation with averaged action logits as the q-value, and the empirical validation of improved performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach by applying Q-learning to LLMs for strategic planning in ESC. While reinforcement learning has been used with LLMs before, the specific application to strategic planning within ESC, using averaged action logits as a Q-value and finetuning based on Bellman equations, is a distinct contribution. The concept of explicitly planning for long-term emotional support using a value-based approach is also a significant advance over typical LLM-based methods which focus on immediate responses. The two distinct reward mechanisms considered are also notable.

*   **Significance:** Emotional support is a critical application area for conversational AI.  The limitations of LLMs in providing consistent and well-planned emotional support are a recognized challenge. By formulating the ESC task as a strategy-level MDP, the paper provides a promising direction for improving the coherence and effectiveness of LLM-driven ESC systems. The experimental results demonstrate that straQ* outperforms various baselines in terms of automatic metrics and human evaluations, suggesting that it has the potential to improve the quality of ESC systems. In particular, the use of GPT-4 scoring to distill knowledge into smaller LLMs appears to be a successful approach and a good design choice.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem formulation and method description.
    *   Novel application of Q-learning to ESC with LLMs.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with multiple datasets and baselines.
    *   Detailed ablation studies and sensitivity analysis.
    *   Exploration of different reward mechanisms.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on human annotations for strategies and GPT-4 for scoring introduces potential biases.
    *   The size of the testsets might be relatively small
    *   The paper should further explain the benefits and drawbacks of each reward mechanism, providing clear directions for reward design choices.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of conversational AI, particularly in the area of emotional support. By demonstrating the effectiveness of value-based strategic planning, it could inspire other researchers to explore similar approaches for improving the long-term coherence and effectiveness of conversational AI systems. The code availability makes it more likely for others to reproduce and build upon the work.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** While the approach is novel, the significance is somewhat limited by the inherent challenges in evaluating emotional support systems. Subjective evaluations are prone to biases, and automatic metrics are not always perfectly correlated with human perception. The paper does a good job of addressing these limitations by using multiple evaluation methods, but the field of ESC evaluation remains a challenge. The potential benefit of Q-learning to ESC can be seen, with potential to improve the quality of a support bot system and make it more reliable.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper makes a solid contribution with a novel method and empirical evidence of effectiveness. It is valuable for improving the support quality and reliability of chatbot systems, while also offering valuable guidance on reward design and model size considerations. The significance is high due to the value of helping support ESC quality, but with the previously specified caveats. Therefore, the score is 7.5.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Replay-Based Continual Learning with Dual-Layered Distillation and a Streamlined U-Net for Efficient Text-to-Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06995v1)
- **Authors**: Md. Naimur Asif Borno, Md Sakib Hossain Shovon, Asmaa Soliman Al-Moisheer, Mohammad Ali Moni
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in text-to-image diffusion models are hindered by high computational demands, limiting accessibility and scalability. This paper introduces KDC-Diff, a novel stable diffusion framework that enhances efficiency while maintaining image quality. KDC-Diff features a streamlined U-Net architecture with nearly half the parameters of the original U-Net (482M), significantly reducing model complexity. We propose a dual-layered distillation strategy to ensure high-fidelity generation, transferring semantic and structural insights from a teacher to a compact student model while minimizing quality degradation. Additionally, replay-based continual learning is integrated to mitigate catastrophic forgetting, allowing the model to retain prior knowledge while adapting to new data. Despite operating under extremely low computational resources, KDC-Diff achieves state-of-the-art performance on the Oxford Flowers and Butterflies & Moths 100 Species datasets, demonstrating competitive metrics such as FID, CLIP, and LPIPS. Moreover, it significantly reduces inference time compared to existing models. These results establish KDC-Diff as a highly efficient and adaptable solution for text-to-image generation, particularly in computationally constrained environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces KDC-Diff, a stable diffusion framework aimed at improving the efficiency of text-to-image generation while preserving image quality.  KDC-Diff incorporates three main components: a streamlined U-Net architecture with significantly fewer parameters than the original, a dual-layered knowledge distillation (KD) strategy to transfer knowledge from a teacher model to the compact student model, and replay-based continual learning (CL) to mitigate catastrophic forgetting.  The authors demonstrate that KDC-Diff achieves state-of-the-art performance on the Oxford Flowers and Butterflies & Moths datasets with competitive FID, CLIP, and LPIPS scores, while also reducing inference time. The paper argues that KDC-Diff offers an efficient and adaptable solution for text-to-image generation, especially in computationally constrained environments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical Problem Addressed:** The paper directly addresses a significant limitation of diffusion models – their high computational cost, making them less accessible.
    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The combination of U-Net streamlining, knowledge distillation, and continual learning is a well-rounded approach to improving efficiency and robustness. The KD strategy incorporates hard target, soft target and feature-based distillation which appears very comprehensive.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The results on the Oxford Flowers and Butterflies & Moths datasets are compelling, demonstrating competitive performance with reduced computational resources. The significant reduction in inference time is a key achievement.
    *   **Reproducibility:**  The paper is fairly detailed in describing the experimental setup and model configurations, which aids reproducibility. Retraining all baselines from scratch is also a plus.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of techniques is effective, each individual technique (U-Net streamlining, KD, CL) is not particularly novel on its own. The paper builds upon existing methods. The UNet changes seem somewhat minor, more like a parameter pruning strategy than a fundamentally new architecture.
    *   **Limited Dataset Scope:** The experiments focus on relatively small and specific datasets. The generalizability to larger, more complex, and diverse datasets is not clearly demonstrated. The images in those datasets tend to be focused on a single object, it will be very helpful to see if the method transfers to broader more diverse datasets.
    *   **Missing Ablation Studies:** Although KD involves hard, soft and feature-based distillation, the paper lacks ablation studies to justify or explore the necessity for the choice of methods. It would be very helpful to understand which components play more significant roles.
    *   **Dependency on Teacher Model:** While knowledge distillation is beneficial, the performance of KDC-Diff is inherently tied to the performance of the teacher model. There's no discussion of how to select the optimal teacher model or the potential impact of a weaker teacher. The approach also inherits the biases of the teacher model.
    *   **Lack of qualitative evaluation**: The paper lacks qualitative evaluation to highlight the superiority of generated images. Although quantitative metrics are useful, the qualitative assessments will be a valuable addition to the model.

*   **Significance:**

The paper offers a practical and effective approach for reducing the computational demands of stable diffusion models. This is significant because it can broaden access to text-to-image generation, particularly for researchers and developers with limited resources. By achieving competitive performance with fewer parameters and reduced inference time, KDC-Diff can contribute to the wider adoption of diffusion models in real-world applications. The framework provides a template for adapting powerful generative models to resource-constrained settings.

*   **Novelty**

The novelty is limited. Although the paper attempts to incorporate three different techniques to solve issues like computational inefficiency, model scalability and catastrophic forgetting, a lot of those are incremental improvements in existing techniques.

**Overall Assessment:**

KDC-Diff represents a valuable contribution to the field by addressing the practical limitations of diffusion models. The approach is well-executed, and the results are compelling. However, the incremental nature of the novelty and the somewhat limited scope of the experiments prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work. The lack of ablation study and qualitative evaluation is also noted.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### CMD: Controllable Multiview Diffusion for 3D Editing and Progressive Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.07003v1)
- **Authors**: Peng Li, Suizhi Ma, Jialiang Chen, Yuan Liu, Chongyi Zhang, Wei Xue, Wenhan Luo, Alla Sheffer, Wenping Wang, Yike Guo
- **Abstract**: Recently, 3D generation methods have shown their powerful ability to automate 3D model creation. However, most 3D generation methods only rely on an input image or a text prompt to generate a 3D model, which lacks the control of each component of the generated 3D model. Any modifications of the input image lead to an entire regeneration of the 3D models. In this paper, we introduce a new method called CMD that generates a 3D model from an input image while enabling flexible local editing of each component of the 3D model. In CMD, we formulate the 3D generation as a conditional multiview diffusion model, which takes the existing or known parts as conditions and generates the edited or added components. This conditional multiview diffusion model not only allows the generation of 3D models part by part but also enables local editing of 3D models according to the local revision of the input image without changing other 3D parts. Extensive experiments are conducted to demonstrate that CMD decomposes a complex 3D generation task into multiple components, improving the generation quality. Meanwhile, CMD enables efficient and flexible local editing of a 3D model by just editing one rendered image.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "CMD: Controllable Multiview Diffusion for 3D Editing and Progressive Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CMD, a new 3D generation method that leverages conditional multiview diffusion to enable both local editing and progressive generation of 3D models. CMD allows users to edit 3D models by modifying a rendered view of the model, and the changes are propagated to the 3D model. The method also supports a progressive generation approach, where complex 3D models are built component by component, with each step conditioned on the previous one. The core of CMD is a conditional multiview diffusion model that takes existing parts as conditions and generates edited components. This allows part-by-part generation and local editing without affecting other parts of the model. The authors demonstrate the method's effectiveness in both local editing and progressive generation tasks, showcasing improved generation quality, flexibility, and efficiency compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of the paper lies in its combination of conditional multiview diffusion with incremental remeshing for controllable 3D editing. The approach of decoupling the 3D editing task into multiview rendering editing followed by 2D-to-3D lifting is a reasonable approach. Extending ControlNet to a multiview setup is an interesting component. The progressive generation scheme with the global conditioning mechanism to maintain coherence is also a valuable contribution. While multiview diffusion models have been explored previously, the application to controllable editing with image-based guidance sets this apart.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an important limitation of existing 3D generation methods, which often lack the ability to perform localized edits or create complex 3D assets in a controlled manner. CMD offers a more intuitive and flexible way to edit 3D models compared to text-prompt-based approaches or manual 3D manipulation. The progressive generation pipeline offers a plausible solution for generating complex scenes, which is difficult for other image-to-3D models.  The speed improvements are significant if the reported 20 seconds is accurate.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Flexibility and Control:** The method provides fine-grained control over the editing process, allowing users to modify specific parts of a 3D model without affecting others.
    *   **Efficiency:**  The paper claims a significant speed advantage over existing methods, making iterative editing more practical.
    *   **High-Quality Results:** Qualitative results demonstrate the ability to generate realistic and high-quality 3D models with complex structures and textures.
    *   **Progressive Generation:** The progressive generation approach provides a viable solution for complex scene generation.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the method and its components.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on External Tools:** The method relies on external image editing tools, which could limit its usability and introduce artifacts if these tools are not perfect. It should have explored implementing its own minimal 2D editing capabilities.
    *   **Topology Preservation:** The incremental reconstruction strategy does not fully preserve the original mesh topology, which could be an issue for some applications (shown in Fig 16).  Also, reliance on "differentiable rendering" for inverse graphics is not ideal as there are faster/more stable techniques possible.
    *   **Limited User Studies:** While a user study is included, a more comprehensive evaluation of the usability and user experience of CMD would be beneficial. The current study has only 20 participants.
    *   **Limited novelty in technical components:** While the whole system might be novel and perform well, the components inside such as usage of diffusion models are not significantly novel compared to existing published research.
    *   **Unclear Datasets:** Description of the dataset needs more clarifications. Specifically how part manipulation and object composition is carried out.

*   **Potential Influence:** CMD has the potential to influence the field of 3D content creation by providing a more intuitive, efficient, and controllable way to generate and edit 3D models. It could be particularly useful for artists, designers, and developers who need to create complex 3D scenes for games, AR/VR applications, or other interactive media.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of 7. The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by addressing an important limitation of existing 3D generation methods, which has good potential for practical applications. While the individual components may not be groundbreaking, the combination of conditional multiview diffusion, and progressive generation scheme is innovative and effective. However, there are also limitations such as dependence on external tools, topological consistency issues, lack of comparison against more recent methods and slightly unclear dataset descriptions, and the methods used in the components are not as technically novel. Considering both the strengths and weaknesses, a score of 7 accurately reflects the paper's overall contribution.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### GuidedQuant: Large Language Model Quantization via Exploiting End Loss Guidance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.07004v1)
- **Authors**: Jinuk Kim, Marwa El Halabi, Wonpyo Park, Clemens JS Schaefer, Deokjae Lee, Yeonhong Park, Jae W. Lee, Hyun Oh Song
- **Abstract**: Post-training quantization is a key technique for reducing the memory and inference latency of large language models by quantizing weights and activations without requiring retraining. However, existing methods either (1) fail to account for the varying importance of hidden features to the end loss or, when incorporating end loss, (2) neglect the critical interactions between model weights. To address these limitations, we propose GuidedQuant, a novel quantization approach that integrates gradient information from the end loss into the quantization objective while preserving cross-weight dependencies within output channels. GuidedQuant consistently boosts the performance of state-of-the-art quantization methods across weight-only scalar, weight-only vector, and weight-and-activation quantization. Additionally, we introduce a novel non-uniform scalar quantization algorithm, which is guaranteed to monotonically decrease the quantization objective value, and outperforms existing methods in this category. We release the code at https://github.com/snu-mllab/GuidedQuant.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "GuidedQuant," a novel post-training quantization (PTQ) approach for large language models (LLMs). GuidedQuant aims to improve existing PTQ methods by incorporating gradient information from the end loss function into the quantization objective. This helps to prioritize the quantization of features that are more important to the model's overall performance.  The key idea is to preserve cross-weight dependencies within output channels during quantization, which existing methods often overlook. The paper also proposes a new non-uniform scalar quantization algorithm called LNQ (Layer-wise Non-uniform Quantization) and demonstrates the effectiveness of GuidedQuant by integrating it with state-of-the-art quantization methods such as QTIP and SpinQuant. The results show consistent performance boosts across different quantization schemes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of GuidedQuant – using gradient information to guide the quantization process while retaining channel-wise dependencies – is a valuable contribution. The block-diagonal Hessian approximation is a known concept, but its adaptation and efficient implementation for large language models, alongside the careful engineering for minimizing storage and computational overhead, show ingenuity. The LNQ algorithm provides an improvement on the 1D variant of GPTVQ for scalar quantization.

**Significance:** The potential impact of GuidedQuant on the field is considerable. The paper demonstrates improved quantization performance across multiple models and quantization methods, indicating general applicability.  A more accurate and efficient PTQ is critical for making LLMs more accessible and deployable on resource-constrained devices.

**Strengths:**

*   **Consistent Performance Improvement:** GuidedQuant consistently improves upon existing state-of-the-art PTQ methods.
*   **Scalability:** The method is designed to be scalable to large models, a crucial factor in the context of LLMs.
*   **Versatility:** Demonstrated effectiveness across different quantization schemes (weight-only scalar, vector, and weight-and-activation).
*   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The paper features comprehensive experimental results on various models and datasets.
*   **Practicality:** The authors address the computational and storage challenges, making the approach more practical for LLM quantization.
*   **Non-uniform Quantization Improvement**: LNQ demonstrably shows the power of more efficient approximations to the k-means problem for quantization.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of gradient guidance and channel-wise dependence in a block-diagonal approximation is innovative, each component separately is known within the general realm of network compression.
*   **Approximation Limitations:** The method still relies on approximations of the Hessian matrix, albeit more accurate than purely diagonal approximations. This limits the extent to which the end loss can be perfectly optimized.
*   **Computational Cost:** While the authors address the cost, backpropagation and Hessian calculation will still add computational expense on top of other algorithms.
*   **Evaluation Scenarios:** While well evaluated on perplexity metrics, it might be valuable to test the impact on real downstream tasks that are more sensitive to subtle changes to model outputs.

**Justification for the Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of 7.

The paper introduces a valuable and practical technique for improving post-training quantization of LLMs. The incremental novelty is acknowledged but is balanced by the significant practical benefits shown by their method in a computationally viable way. The method's versatility and experimental rigor enhance its value. While the reliance on approximations still limits optimal performance, the results demonstrate a significant step forward in efficient and effective LLM quantization. The impact of enabling broader deployment of quantized LLMs justifies a strong score, although the incremental contribution rather than a paradigm shift prevents a higher one.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### MELLM: Exploring LLM-Powered Micro-Expression Understanding Enhanced by Subtle Motion Perception
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.07007v1)
- **Authors**: Zhengye Zhang, Sirui Zhao, Shifeng Liu, Shukang Yin, Xinglong Mao, Tong Xu, Enhong Chen
- **Abstract**: Micro-expressions (MEs) are crucial psychological responses with significant potential for affective computing. However, current automatic micro-expression recognition (MER) research primarily focuses on discrete emotion classification, neglecting a convincing analysis of the subtle dynamic movements and inherent emotional cues. The rapid progress in multimodal large language models (MLLMs), known for their strong multimodal comprehension and language generation abilities, offers new possibilities. MLLMs have shown success in various vision-language tasks, indicating their potential to understand MEs comprehensively, including both fine-grained motion patterns and underlying emotional semantics. Nevertheless, challenges remain due to the subtle intensity and short duration of MEs, as existing MLLMs are not designed to capture such delicate frame-level facial dynamics. In this paper, we propose a novel Micro-Expression Large Language Model (MELLM), which incorporates a subtle facial motion perception strategy with the strong inference capabilities of MLLMs, representing the first exploration of MLLMs in the domain of ME analysis. Specifically, to explicitly guide the MLLM toward motion-sensitive regions, we construct an interpretable motion-enhanced color map by fusing onset-apex optical flow dynamics with the corresponding grayscale onset frame as the model input. Additionally, specialized fine-tuning strategies are incorporated to further enhance the model's visual perception of MEs. Furthermore, we construct an instruction-description dataset based on Facial Action Coding System (FACS) annotations and emotion labels to train our MELLM. Comprehensive evaluations across multiple benchmark datasets demonstrate that our model exhibits superior robustness and generalization capabilities in ME understanding (MEU). Code is available at https://github.com/zyzhangUstc/MELLM.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "MELLM: Exploring LLM-Powered Micro-Expression Understanding Enhanced by Subtle Motion Perception" introduces a novel approach to micro-expression understanding (MEU) by leveraging the capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Recognizing the limitations of existing MLLMs in capturing subtle facial dynamics, the authors propose MELLM, a framework that incorporates a motion-enhanced color mapping (MMC-Mapping) technique to highlight motion-sensitive regions in facial images. This is combined with a flow-guided micro-expression understanding (FGMU) strategy to align optical flow features with action unit (AU) annotations.  The authors fine-tune the Qwen2.5-VL MLLM using a newly constructed instruction-following MEU dataset and demonstrate improved performance on various benchmark datasets compared to general MER methods and other MLLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its exploration of MLLMs for the MEU task. While MLLMs have been used in affective computing for macro-expressions and general emotion recognition, their application to the more nuanced and subtle domain of micro-expressions is a relatively unexplored area. The proposed MMC-Mapping and FGMU strategies, while not entirely revolutionary, represent a sensible approach to bridge the gap between low-level motion features and high-level emotional interpretations for MLLMs. Creating an instruction-following MEU dataset specifically tailored for training MLLMs is also a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** Micro-expression analysis has significant applications in various fields, and improving automated MEU systems is crucial. The paper attempts to address the limitations of current MER methods by providing a more interpretable and comprehensive understanding of MEs, moving beyond simple emotion classification. The integration of MLLMs could potentially lead to more robust and generalizable MEU systems.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses an important and challenging problem in affective computing.
    *   Presents a well-defined framework (MELLM) with clear components (MMC-Mapping, FGMU, instruction-based fine-tuning).
    *   Demonstrates improved performance compared to baselines and other MLLMs on multiple benchmark datasets.
    *   Introduces a new MEU dataset, which contributes to the research community.
    *   Provides a case study showcasing the potential of MELLM for interpretable MEU.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The gains over specialized MER methods, while present, are relatively modest on the DFME dataset, which is the only dataset the model is specifically trained on. The case study highlights the *potential* for more interpretable results but is still somewhat qualitative. The paper doesn't provide significant comparative analyses on the interpretability of the results beyond the case study.

    *   The paper leans heavily on the pre-trained capabilities of Qwen2.5-VL. The actual architectural contributions are relatively light (LoRA fine-tuning). While effective, the method is somewhat incremental. The MMC-Mapping, while intuitive, isn't rigorously justified with ablation studies or alternative designs explored.
    *   The study is focused on a limited number of emotions due to constraints of existing datasets.  The generalization of the approach to a broader spectrum of emotional states remains an open question.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could potentially influence the field by encouraging further research on MLLMs for MEU. The proposed framework and dataset may serve as a starting point for future investigations. The interpretable approach could lead to more trustworthy and reliable MEU systems. However, the impact will depend on whether the gains can be further improved and whether the approach proves to be truly generalizable across different datasets, emotions, and application scenarios.

**Score Rationale:**

While the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by exploring MLLMs for MEU and proposing a reasonable framework, its novelty is somewhat limited. The results, while promising, are not overwhelmingly superior to existing methods. The technical contributions are mainly incremental, relying on existing pre-trained models and established techniques. The interpretability aspect, while highlighted, isn't rigorously evaluated. The limitations, while adequately discussed, warrant a slightly lower score.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### LLM-Augmented Chemical Synthesis and Design Decision Programs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.07027v1)
- **Authors**: Haorui Wang, Jeff Guo, Lingkai Kong, Rampi Ramprasad, Philippe Schwaller, Yuanqi Du, Chao Zhang
- **Abstract**: Retrosynthesis, the process of breaking down a target molecule into simpler precursors through a series of valid reactions, stands at the core of organic chemistry and drug development. Although recent machine learning (ML) research has advanced single-step retrosynthetic modeling and subsequent route searches, these solutions remain restricted by the extensive combinatorial space of possible pathways. Concurrently, large language models (LLMs) have exhibited remarkable chemical knowledge, hinting at their potential to tackle complex decision-making tasks in chemistry. In this work, we explore whether LLMs can successfully navigate the highly constrained, multi-step retrosynthesis planning problem. We introduce an efficient scheme for encoding reaction pathways and present a new route-level search strategy, moving beyond the conventional step-by-step reactant prediction. Through comprehensive evaluations, we show that our LLM-augmented approach excels at retrosynthesis planning and extends naturally to the broader challenge of synthesizable molecular design.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the potential of Large Language Models (LLMs) for retrosynthesis planning and synthesizable molecular design. Unlike previous LLM-based retrosynthesis approaches that often involve fine-tuning, this work investigates how LLMs can be leveraged *without* training, focusing on their inherent chemical knowledge and reasoning abilities under constrained decision-making scenarios. The authors introduce an efficient scheme for encoding reaction pathways and a novel route-level search strategy, moving beyond traditional step-by-step reactant prediction.  They propose an evolutionary search algorithm, termed LLM-Syn-Planner, which uses the LLM to generate full retrosynthetic routes, guided by an evolutionary algorithm and partial rewards.  The method is tested on standard retrosynthesis benchmark datasets (USPTO, Pistachio) and in synthesizable molecular design, where the framework integrates LLMs as molecular structure optimizers ensuring the resulting molecules are synthesizable.  The results show that the LLM-Syn-Planner outperforms LLMs used for single-step prediction within search algorithms and achieves comparable (or better) performance than traditional retrosynthesis tools. Ablation studies highlight the importance of linear pathway encoding, partial rewards, and intermediate feedback.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel elements:

    *   **Un-tuned LLM Approach:** The primary novelty lies in the deliberate choice to avoid fine-tuning the LLM and instead explore its *intrinsic* chemical knowledge for retrosynthesis. This contrasts with existing works that often rely on fine-tuning or instruction tuning.
    *   **Route-Level Planning:** The shift from step-by-step prediction to LLM-generated *full route* planning is a significant departure from traditional retrosynthesis approaches. The evolutionary algorithm then provides a mechanism to refine these routes.
    *   **Encoding Scheme:** The proposed linear format for encoding synthesis routes, as opposed to more complex tree structures, is well-suited to LLM processing.
    *   **Partial Reward System:**  Addressing the sparse reward problem with a partial reward system using SC score, providing better feedback for the LLM to improve pathways.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to:

    *   **Unlock LLM Capabilities:** Demonstrate the hidden abilities of pre-trained LLMs in complex, constraint-driven chemical tasks.
    *   **Simplify Retrosynthesis Workflows:** Offer a potentially simpler and more accessible approach to retrosynthesis, without the need for extensive model training.
    *   **Inform Future Research:** Guide future research directions towards LLM-based full-route planning and optimization, rather than solely focusing on single-step prediction.
    *   **Synthesizable Molecular Design:** Demonstrating the framework can handle both retrosynthesis and optimizing molecular properties.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-Defined Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly frames retrosynthesis and synthesizable design as sequential decision-making problems amenable to LLM-based solutions.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study includes thorough experiments on standard datasets and ablation studies to validate the contributions of each component.
    *   **Clear Explanations:** The methodology is explained in detail, and the results are presented in a clear and accessible manner.
    *   **Addresses Key Challenges:** The paper tackles the key challenges of retrosynthesis, such as sparse rewards, chemical validity, and the combinatorial explosion of possible routes.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Existing Tools:** While the LLM is used for planning, the evaluation heavily relies on existing tools like RDKit for validity checks and databases for commercially available compounds and reaction mappings. The novelty is in how the LLM orchestrates these elements, not in replacing them.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the authors set a 60-minute time limit, the practical computational cost of using LLMs for each iteration of the evolutionary algorithm could be high for real-world applications.
    *   **Generalizability:**  The experiments are performed on specific datasets (USPTO, Pistachio). It remains to be seen how well the approach generalizes to more diverse or complex chemical spaces.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The work could influence researchers to explore the capabilities of pre-trained LLMs in chemistry tasks beyond simple prediction.
    *   The route-level planning approach could lead to the development of more efficient and scalable retrosynthesis tools.
    *   The use of LLMs in synthesizable molecular design has the potential to accelerate the discovery of novel therapeutic and materials.

*   **Score:** 7

*   **Justification:** The paper offers a significant contribution to the field by demonstrating the potential of untrained LLMs for complex chemical tasks. The route-level planning and evolutionary search strategy are novel and effective. The study is well-designed and comprehensively evaluated. However, the reliance on existing tools and the potential computational cost limit the immediate impact. While the work opens promising avenues for future research, it has room for improvement in terms of generalizability and computational efficiency. The score reflects the paper's solid novelty and good potential significance, with some limitations.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### DAPE: Dual-Stage Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning for Consistent Video Editing with Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.07057v1)
- **Authors**: Junhao Xia, Chaoyang Zhang, Yecheng Zhang, Chengyang Zhou, Zhichang Wang, Bochun Liu, Dongshuo Yin
- **Abstract**: Video generation based on diffusion models presents a challenging multimodal task, with video editing emerging as a pivotal direction in this field. Recent video editing approaches primarily fall into two categories: training-required and training-free methods. While training-based methods incur high computational costs, training-free alternatives often yield suboptimal performance. To address these limitations, we propose DAPE, a high-quality yet cost-effective two-stage parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) framework for video editing. In the first stage, we design an efficient norm-tuning method to enhance temporal consistency in generated videos. The second stage introduces a vision-friendly adapter to improve visual quality. Additionally, we identify critical shortcomings in existing benchmarks, including limited category diversity, imbalanced object distribution, and inconsistent frame counts. To mitigate these issues, we curate a large dataset benchmark comprising 232 videos with rich annotations and 6 editing prompts, enabling objective and comprehensive evaluation of advanced methods. Extensive experiments on existing datasets (BalanceCC, LOVEU-TGVE, RAVE) and our proposed benchmark demonstrate that DAPE significantly improves temporal coherence and text-video alignment while outperforming previous state-of-the-art approaches.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the DAPE paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "DAPE: Dual-Stage Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning for Consistent Video Editing with Diffusion Models" introduces a novel framework for text-guided video editing that aims to improve both temporal consistency and visual quality while remaining computationally efficient.  The framework consists of two stages: 1) Adjustable Norm-tuning: Normalization layers are fine-tuned with learnable scale factors to improve temporal consistency, particularly sensitive to normalization scale within temporal layers. 2) Adapter-tuning: visual adapter modules are strategically integrated into a diffusion model to enhance single-video template comprehension, addressing the limited-training data problem of video template editing. The authors also identify limitations in existing video editing benchmarks and introduce a new, large-scale, high-quality dataset called DAPE Dataset for more comprehensive evaluation. Experiments across multiple datasets demonstrate that DAPE outperforms existing methods in both quantitative metrics and qualitative results. The study investigates how PEFT methods influence temporal consistency and text-video alignment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of a two-stage PEFT approach tailored for the specific challenges of video editing using diffusion models has merit. The adjustable norm-tuning for temporal consistency and the visually-focused adapter modules demonstrate a good level of consideration of the video generation task.  The authors highlight a specific issue in maintaining temporal consistency using the normalization scales, which appears to be a novel finding. While parameter-efficient fine-tuning is a well-established technique, applying it specifically to video editing and creating the two-stage structure with different objectives in each stage is a valuable step.

* **Significance:** Improving the quality and efficiency of video editing is a practically important problem. The paper's focus on generating consistent videos with a lower computational cost could make video editing more accessible. However, video editing is improving rapidly and thus, the impact will depend on relative performance advantages. The introduction of DAPE Dataset fills a need in the research community for better benchmarks, especially regarding diversity and standardization.  A well constructed and annotated dataset is valuable even in the long run.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem definition:**  The paper clearly identifies the trade-off between quality and computational cost in video editing and positions its work to address this.
    *   **Technical approach:** The dual-stage framework is well-motivated and technically sound, with specific designs for temporal consistency and visual quality.  The ablation studies convincingly support the necessity of the dual-stage training.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The experiments are extensive, covering multiple datasets and ablation studies, providing strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed method.
    *   **Dataset contribution:** The DAPE Dataset addresses the limitations of existing benchmarks. Its creation demonstrates a commitment to rigorous evaluation and advancement of the field. The annotation complexity and consideration of visual aspects such as camera angle is helpful.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental advance:** Although technically solid, the approach can be seen as an incremental improvement upon existing video editing methodologies. The technical contributions aren’t fundamentally new when considering the large body of work on PEFT methods.
    *   **Limited scope of edits:** The types of edits addressed are fairly standard. The complexity is more in ensuring video consistency, rather than facilitating more complex user edits. The paper doesn’t address some of the more challenging editing scenarios.
    *   **Reliance on existing models:** The method builds upon existing diffusion models. While this is a common approach, it does limit the potential for transformative results compared to developing completely novel video generation architectures.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in video editing and generation, particularly concerning the application of PEFT techniques and the importance of well-designed benchmarks. DAPE Dataset should become a valuable resource for comparing new methods. The insights in temporal consistency and design of visual PEFT modules will also likely influence future works.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper is technically sound and presents a useful contribution to the field of video editing with diffusion models. The combination of parameter-efficient fine-tuning with a focus on temporal consistency and the creation of a high-quality dataset are valuable assets. However, the approach is somewhat incremental, and the results, while impressive, might not be viewed as ground-breaking. The impact is further tempered by the rapid advancements in this field, but that doesn't detract from the merits of this paper.

The score reflects the paper's strong experimental results, solid technical execution and contribution to the benchmark datasets. Although the algorithmic advance is incremental, it still tackles a hard problem in a worthwhile manner.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### ParaView-MCP: An Autonomous Visualization Agent with Direct Tool Use
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.07064v1)
- **Authors**: Shusen Liu, Haichao Miao, Peer-Timo Bremer
- **Abstract**: While powerful and well-established, tools like ParaView present a steep learning curve that discourages many potential users. This work introduces ParaView-MCP, an autonomous agent that integrates modern multimodal large language models (MLLMs) with ParaView to not only lower the barrier to entry but also augment ParaView with intelligent decision support. By leveraging the state-of-the-art reasoning, command execution, and vision capabilities of MLLMs, ParaView-MCP enables users to interact with ParaView through natural language and visual inputs. Specifically, our system adopted the Model Context Protocol (MCP) - a standardized interface for model-application communication - that facilitates direct interaction between MLLMs with ParaView's Python API to allow seamless information exchange between the user, the language model, and the visualization tool itself. Furthermore, by implementing a visual feedback mechanism that allows the agent to observe the viewport, we unlock a range of new capabilities, including recreating visualizations from examples, closed-loop visualization parameter updates based on user-defined goals, and even cross-application collaboration involving multiple tools. Broadly, we believe such an agent-driven visualization paradigm can profoundly change the way we interact with visualization tools. We expect a significant uptake in the development of such visualization tools, in both visualization research and industry.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ParaView-MCP, an autonomous visualization agent that integrates a multimodal large language model (MLLM) with the ParaView visualization platform.  The system leverages the Model Context Protocol (MCP) for communication between the MLLM and ParaView's Python API.  ParaView-MCP enables users to interact with ParaView through natural language and visual inputs, lowering the barrier to entry for less experienced users and potentially augmenting ParaView with intelligent decision support. The agent can recreate visualizations from examples, update parameters based on user-defined goals, and facilitate cross-application collaboration.  The paper presents example use cases and preliminary feedback from domain experts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a compelling application of MLLMs for enhancing the usability of a complex scientific visualization tool.  The integration with ParaView via MCP represents a practical approach, enabling direct tool manipulation and closing the loop with visual feedback.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty in Integration:** While individual components (MLLMs, ParaView, MCP) are not novel, their integration into a functional and accessible visualization agent is a significant contribution. The hybrid approach of using API control coupled with focused visual understanding avoids some of the pitfalls of pure vision-based or command-line approaches.
*   **Practical Application:**  The work focuses on a real-world problem (the steep learning curve of scientific visualization tools) and provides a concrete solution with ParaView-MCP.  The demonstration through example use cases helps to illustrate the potential benefits.
*   **Improved Accessibility:** The core value proposition is to make a powerful tool more accessible to non-experts, which is a worthy goal.
*   **Clear System Architecture:** The paper clearly outlines the system's components (ParaView Manager, MCP Server) and their roles in facilitating communication and command execution.
*   **Emphasis on reproducibility:** The emphasis on reproducible code, in opposition to direct code generation, helps the long term validity of the tool

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Evaluation:** The evaluation relies primarily on anecdotal feedback from two domain experts. A more rigorous evaluation with a larger user group and quantitative metrics (e.g., task completion time, user satisfaction) would strengthen the claims. The tool is also only evaluated with Claude. How other MLLMs would perform has not been checked.
*   **Dependency on Specific MLLM:** The system's performance is tied to the capabilities of the chosen MLLM (Claude's Sonnet 3.7). The discussion acknowledges limitations with this model, highlighting the need for more robust visual reasoning.  The MCP integration helps to mitigate this issue by supporting different model architectures.
*   **Scalability and Complexity:**  While the paper addresses direct tool use, the underlying complexity of ParaView and the need for a curated set of functionalities within the ParaView Manager could limit the system's scalability to more complex visualization tasks or a wider range of applications.
*   **Potential for Misinterpretation:** It also does not address the situation where the user can misinterpret the visualizations made and provided to them by the MLLM.

**Significance and Impact:**

The paper's significance lies in its potential to democratize scientific visualization.  If ParaView-MCP can demonstrably lower the barrier to entry and empower domain scientists to create high-quality visualizations without extensive training, it could have a substantial impact on research workflows.  The work also provides a valuable blueprint for integrating MLLMs with other complex software tools.

**Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and practical integration of MLLMs with a well-established visualization platform. The system has the potential to improve the accessibility of scientific visualization. However, the evaluation is limited, and the system's reliance on a specific MLLM and potential scalability issues need to be addressed.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Scaling Laws and Representation Learning in Simple Hierarchical Languages: Transformers vs. Convolutional Architectures
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.07070v1)
- **Authors**: Francesco Cagnetta, Alessandro Favero, Antonio Sclocchi, Matthieu Wyart
- **Abstract**: How do neural language models acquire a language's structure when trained for next-token prediction? We address this question by deriving theoretical scaling laws for neural network performance on synthetic datasets generated by the Random Hierarchy Model (RHM) -- an ensemble of probabilistic context-free grammars designed to capture the hierarchical structure of natural language while remaining analytically tractable. Previously, we developed a theory of representation learning based on data correlations that explains how deep learning models capture the hierarchical structure of the data sequentially, one layer at a time. Here, we extend our theoretical framework to account for architectural differences. In particular, we predict and empirically validate that convolutional networks, whose structure aligns with that of the generative process through locality and weight sharing, enjoy a faster scaling of performance compared to transformer models, which rely on global self-attention mechanisms. This finding clarifies the architectural biases underlying neural scaling laws and highlights how representation learning is shaped by the interaction between model architecture and the statistical properties of data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how different neural network architectures (Transformers and Convolutional Neural Networks - CNNs) learn hierarchical compositional data.  The authors use the Random Hierarchy Model (RHM), a synthetic dataset with a known hierarchical structure, to train both types of networks. They derive theoretical scaling laws for the performance of these networks and empirically validate these laws by observing the learning dynamics.  The key finding is that CNNs, whose structure aligns better with the RHM data due to locality and weight sharing, exhibit faster learning and scaling compared to Transformers, which rely on global self-attention mechanisms. The paper also explores the internal representations learned by these networks, showing how they progressively capture the hierarchical structure of the data. Finally, they analyze locally connected networks to disentangle the contributions of local connectivity and weight sharing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper builds upon the authors' previous work on the RHM and representation learning.  The key novelty lies in extending the theoretical framework to account for architectural differences and in providing a direct comparison between Transformers and CNNs on this controlled synthetic dataset. The exploration of locally connected networks to isolate the role of weight sharing is also a significant addition. While the use of synthetic data to understand learning dynamics is not new in itself, the careful design of the RHM and the specific architectural comparisons offer fresh insights.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its contribution to understanding the relationship between model architecture, data structure, and scaling laws. While neural scaling laws are well-documented empirically, theoretical explanations are still limited. This work provides a concrete example of how architectural biases can influence learning efficiency and scaling behavior in a hierarchical setting. The findings help clarify which architectural priors might be better suited to particular data structures, which could be valuable in guiding architecture selection for real-world tasks.  The analysis of internal representations and how they evolve during training provides further insight into how these models learn compositional data.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong Theoretical Grounding:** The paper provides a well-developed theoretical framework, based on data correlations, that leads to specific predictions about scaling laws.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The theoretical predictions are rigorously tested and validated using empirical experiments on both Transformers and CNNs.
    * **Controlled Setting:** The use of synthetic data allows for precise control over the data structure and enables analytical tractability.
    * **Clear and Well-Organized:** The paper is clearly written and well-organized, making it relatively easy to follow the theoretical arguments and empirical results.
    * **Careful Isolation of Effects:** The inclusion of locally connected networks is crucial for disentangling the effects of local connectivity and weight sharing.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Synthetic Data:** The main limitation is the use of synthetic data.  While the RHM is designed to capture some aspects of natural language, it's still a simplified model.  It's not clear how well these results generalize to real-world datasets with more complex dependencies and irregularities.
    * **Simplified Architectures:**  The Transformer and CNN architectures used in the paper are relatively simple compared to state-of-the-art models. This is necessary for analytical tractability, but it might limit the applicability of the findings to more complex architectures.
    * **Limited Task:**  The last-token prediction task, while sufficient for studying learning dynamics, is also a simplification of more complex language modeling tasks.

* **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the field by:
    * **Guiding Architecture Design:**  Providing insights into which architectural biases are better suited for hierarchical data.
    * **Inspiring Further Theoretical Work:** Encouraging further theoretical analysis of scaling laws and representation learning in more complex data structures and architectures.
    * **Informing Empirical Studies:** Suggesting specific experiments for evaluating the performance of different architectures on real-world datasets.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to understanding the interplay between architecture, data structure, and scaling laws. The theoretical framework is well-developed, and the empirical validation is rigorous. However, the use of synthetic data and simplified architectures limits the immediate applicability of the findings to real-world scenarios. While the paper adds to the body of knowledge of the area, its limited application restricts its value. Thus, a score of 6 reflects the paper's combination of theoretical and empirical rigor with a moderate degree of generalizability.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Semantic-Guided Diffusion Model for Single-Step Image Super-Resolution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.07071v1)
- **Authors**: Zihang Liu, Zhenyu Zhang, Hao Tang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion-based image super-resolution (SR) methods have demonstrated remarkable performance. Recent advancements have introduced deterministic sampling processes that reduce inference from 15 iterative steps to a single step, thereby significantly improving the inference speed of existing diffusion models. However, their efficiency remains limited when handling complex semantic regions due to the single-step inference. To address this limitation, we propose SAMSR, a semantic-guided diffusion framework that incorporates semantic segmentation masks into the sampling process. Specifically, we introduce the SAM-Noise Module, which refines Gaussian noise using segmentation masks to preserve spatial and semantic features. Furthermore, we develop a pixel-wise sampling strategy that dynamically adjusts the residual transfer rate and noise strength based on pixel-level semantic weights, prioritizing semantically rich regions during the diffusion process. To enhance model training, we also propose a semantic consistency loss, which aligns pixel-wise semantic weights between predictions and ground truth. Extensive experiments on both real-world and synthetic datasets demonstrate that SAMSR significantly improves perceptual quality and detail recovery, particularly in semantically complex images. Our code is released at https://github.com/Liu-Zihang/SAMSR.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Semantic-Guided Diffusion Model for Single-Step Image Super-Resolution":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes SAMSR, a new diffusion-based image super-resolution (SR) framework that incorporates semantic guidance through segmentation masks. SAMSR addresses the limitations of single-step SR methods, which struggle with complex semantic regions. The framework uses a SAM-Noise Module to refine Gaussian noise based on segmentation masks and a pixel-wise sampling strategy that dynamically adjusts residual transfer rate and noise strength based on semantic weights. A semantic consistency loss is also introduced to improve training. The authors demonstrate improved perceptual quality and detail recovery compared to existing methods on both real-world and synthetic datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the integration of semantic segmentation within a single-step diffusion SR framework. Specifically, these aspects contribute to its novelty:

    *   **SAM-Noise Module:** The idea of refining Gaussian noise using segmentation masks to preserve spatial and semantic features is a novel approach. It is a step forward from uniform noise priors.
    *   **Pixel-wise Sampling Strategy:** Dynamically adjusting residual transfer rate and noise strength based on pixel-level semantic weights is a unique way to prioritize semantically rich regions during diffusion.
    *   **Semantic Consistency Loss:** Enforcing consistency between semantic weights of predictions and ground truth is a good way to leverage semantic information.

    While semantic information has been utilized in diffusion models before, the specific way it's integrated into a *single-step* diffusion SR process, and the design of the SAM-Noise Module, provide a unique contribution. The prior art, notably HoliSDiP, used semantic information, but this paper's approach to single-step SR offers a different avenue for improvement.
*   **Significance:**

    *   **Improved Perceptual Quality and Detail Recovery:** The experimental results indicate SAMSR performs better at detail recovery and perceptual quality, especially in semantically complex areas. This is significant because it addresses a weakness of existing fast SR methods.
    *   **Single-Step Efficiency:** The paper builds upon single-step methods, aiming for speed. If the performance gains claimed are achieved with minimal computational overhead, this is highly valuable.
    *   **Practical Applicability:** The code release enhances the impact, allowing others to build upon and evaluate this method.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing single-step diffusion SR methods in handling semantically complex regions.
    *   **Well-Defined Solution:** SAMSR is a well-structured framework with specific modules (SAM-Noise Module, pixel-wise sampling) designed to address the identified problem.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper presents results on both real-world and synthetic datasets, using appropriate evaluation metrics.
    *   **Semantic Consistency Loss:** Utilizing a semantic consistency loss to promote a better understanding of the segmentation masks during training contributes to the model's ability to reconstruct finer details.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on SAM:** The method's performance is strongly dependent on the quality of the segmentation provided by the Segment Anything Model (SAM). Errors or inaccuracies in SAM's segmentation will likely propagate through the SR process. The robustness in situations with noisy or inaccurate segmentation outputs needs to be more explicitly discussed. How about if the segmentation masks are erroneous, what will be the model performance in that scenario?
    *   **Limited Ablation Study:** While the authors perform an ablation study comparing various combinations of hyperparameters, the effectiveness of using multiple different semantic region noises is not studied.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   SAMSR's approach of integrating semantic information into single-step SR could inspire further research in this area.
    *   The SAM-Noise Module and pixel-wise sampling strategy could be adapted and applied to other image processing tasks.
    *   The release of code and models will facilitate adoption and further development by the research community.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and potentially impactful approach to single-step image super-resolution. The integration of semantic guidance addresses a key limitation of existing fast SR methods. The proposed SAMSR framework is well-defined and the experimental results demonstrate improved performance. However, the method's reliance on SAM introduces a potential point of failure, and certain aspects of the evaluation are not detailed enough.

Overall, it's a solid contribution to the field, but needs some further robustness analysis and detail in the study of other hyperparameters.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Can LLM-based Financial Investing Strategies Outperform the Market in Long Run?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.07078v1)
- **Authors**: Weixian Waylon Li, Hyeonjun Kim, Mihai Cucuringu, Tiejun Ma
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently been leveraged for asset pricing tasks and stock trading applications, enabling AI agents to generate investment decisions from unstructured financial data. However, most evaluations of LLM timing-based investing strategies are conducted on narrow timeframes and limited stock universes, overstating effectiveness due to survivorship and data-snooping biases. We critically assess their generalizability and robustness by proposing FINSABER, a backtesting framework evaluating timing-based strategies across longer periods and a larger universe of symbols. Systematic backtests over two decades and 100+ symbols reveal that previously reported LLM advantages deteriorate significantly under broader cross-section and over a longer-term evaluation. Our market regime analysis further demonstrates that LLM strategies are overly conservative in bull markets, underperforming passive benchmarks, and overly aggressive in bear markets, incurring heavy losses. These findings highlight the need to develop LLM strategies that are able to prioritise trend detection and regime-aware risk controls over mere scaling of framework complexity.
- **Summary**: This paper, titled "Can LLM-based Financial Investing Strategies Outperform the Market in Long Run?", critically assesses the generalizability and robustness of Large Language Model (LLM)-based financial investing strategies. The authors introduce FINSABER, a backtesting framework designed to evaluate timing-based strategies over extended periods and a larger universe of symbols while mitigating survivorship, look-ahead, and data-snooping biases. Through systematic backtests spanning two decades and involving over 100 symbols, the study reveals that previously reported advantages of LLM-driven approaches deteriorate significantly under broader cross-sectional and longer-term evaluations. The paper further demonstrates that LLM strategies tend to be overly conservative in bull markets and excessively aggressive in bear markets, resulting in suboptimal performance and significant losses. Based on these findings, the authors advocate for the development of LLM strategies that prioritize trend detection and regime-aware risk controls over framework complexity.

**Novelty and Significance Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a critical gap in the existing literature on LLM-based financial investing. While numerous studies have explored the potential of LLMs for asset pricing and trading, many suffer from methodological flaws, including short evaluation periods, limited symbol coverage, and inadequate bias mitigation. The introduction of FINSABER represents a valuable contribution by providing a comprehensive and rigorous framework for benchmarking these strategies. The authors explicitly address survivorship bias, look-ahead bias, and data-snooping bias, which are frequently overlooked in previous work.

The core finding that LLM advantages often vanish under more robust evaluation conditions is significant. It challenges the overly optimistic conclusions of some recent studies and highlights the importance of careful backtesting and bias mitigation in quantitative finance. The regime-specific analysis, which reveals the conservatism of LLM strategies in bull markets and their aggressiveness in bear markets, offers valuable insights into the limitations of current LLM-based approaches.

However, the paper has some limitations:

1.  **Limited Scope of LLM Strategies:** The paper primarily focuses on timing-based strategies. While timing is an essential aspect of investing, it doesn't capture the full spectrum of LLM applications in finance, such as portfolio allocation, risk management, or fraud detection. A broader analysis encompassing these areas would be more comprehensive.
2.  **Complexity of Parameter Selection:**  The parameterization of classical rules and traditional approaches might not be optimal and could create an unfair baseline. Although the authors explicitly mention that the intention is to show the limitations of the tested LLMs, the selected classical methods' setting could have been more rigorous.
3.  **Lack of Interpretability in LLM Decisions:** Although FINSABER provides robust evaluation, the paper doesn't deeply analyze *why* the LLMs perform poorly in certain regimes. Understanding the specific reasoning flaws of LLMs could guide future research in more effective design.
4.  **Cost Analysis:** While the cost analysis is a valuable addition, a more detailed breakdown of the cost drivers could be beneficial. For example, comparing the cost of different API endpoints or exploring alternative LLM deployment options would be useful.
5.  **Limited Number of Trading Strategies.** A limited number of open-source trading LLM strategies are considered (only two)

Despite these limitations, the paper's contributions are substantial. The FINSABER framework is a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners, and the empirical findings provide important insights into the challenges and opportunities of LLM-based financial investing. The emphasis on trend detection and regime-aware risk controls is a promising direction for future research.

**Score: 7.5**

Justification: The paper's novelty lies in its rigorous and comprehensive evaluation of LLM-based investing strategies using a novel backtesting framework that addresses critical biases. The findings that reported LLM advantages often disappear under broader and longer evaluations, and the detailed regime-specific analysis, are significant contributions to the field. While the scope of LLM strategies could be broader, and a deeper dive into the "why" behind the LLM shortcomings could improve interpretability, the paper provides a much-needed dose of realism to the hype surrounding LLMs in finance, and its explicit focus on robustness, bias mitigation, and long-term performance represents a valuable step forward. The emphasis on the economic considerations and the use of a cost analysis also help provide a practical view. Therefore, a score of 7.5 reflects the valuable contribution of the paper and provides a basis for improving the framework in future research.

- **Classification**: q-fin.TR
- **Score**: 7/10

### DriveSOTIF: Advancing Perception SOTIF Through Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.07084v1)
- **Authors**: Shucheng Huang, Freda Shi, Chen Sun, Jiaming Zhong, Minghao Ning, Yufeng Yang, Yukun Lu, Hong Wang, Amir Khajepour
- **Abstract**: Human drivers naturally possess the ability to perceive driving scenarios, predict potential hazards, and react instinctively due to their spatial and causal intelligence, which allows them to perceive, understand, predict, and interact with the 3D world both spatially and temporally. Autonomous vehicles, however, lack these capabilities, leading to challenges in effectively managing perception-related Safety of the Intended Functionality (SOTIF) risks, particularly in complex and unpredictable driving conditions. To address this gap, we propose an approach that fine-tunes multimodal language models (MLLMs) on a customized dataset specifically designed to capture perception-related SOTIF scenarios. Model benchmarking demonstrates that this tailored dataset enables the models to better understand and respond to these complex driving situations. Additionally, in real-world case studies, the proposed method correctly handles challenging scenarios that even human drivers may find difficult. Real-time performance tests further indicate the potential for the models to operate efficiently in live driving environments. This approach, along with the dataset generation pipeline, shows significant promise for improving the identification, cognition, prediction, and reaction to SOTIF-related risks in autonomous driving systems. The dataset and information are available: https://github.com/s95huang/DriveSOTIF.git
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "DriveSOTIF: Advancing Perception SOTIF Through Multimodal Large Language Models" addresses the challenge of managing perception-related Safety of the Intended Functionality (SOTIF) risks in autonomous vehicles.  The authors argue that current methods (rule-based and learning-based) struggle with adaptability, interpretability, and robustness in dynamic driving environments. To overcome these limitations, the paper proposes fine-tuning multimodal language models (MLLMs) on a custom-designed dataset, named DriveSOTIF, that captures diverse, safety-critical driving scenarios.  The fine-tuned MLLMs are then benchmarked and validated in both simulated and real-world settings to demonstrate their ability to detect, reason about, and mitigate perception-related SOTIF risks. The key contributions include the integration of MLLMs into SOTIF research, the creation of the DriveSOTIF dataset, and the deployment/validation of the fine-tuned models. The authors show through qualitative and quantitative analysis that the fine-tuned models outperform baseline models in image captioning and visual question answering (VQA) tasks related to SOTIF, enhancing risk identification and situational awareness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of leveraging MLLMs to address perception-related SOTIF risks in autonomous driving is relatively novel. While previous work has explored LLMs for AD, this paper specifically focuses on SOTIF and employs a custom dataset to fine-tune the MLLMs for this purpose. The DriveSOTIF dataset itself is a valuable contribution, providing a resource for training and evaluating models on SOTIF-related scenarios. However, the application of supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is a well-established technique, and the MLLM architectures used are existing models. Therefore, the novelty primarily lies in the specific application and the dataset.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses an important problem in autonomous driving – ensuring safety in situations where the perception system, although functioning correctly, misinterprets the driving environment. The ability of MLLMs to reason about and respond to safety-critical situations holds significant potential for improving the robustness and reliability of autonomous vehicles. The qualitative and quantitative results presented demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach in handling complex scenarios that even human drivers may find challenging. This has implications for the future development of autonomous driving systems and safety standards.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing approaches to perception SOTIF and motivates the use of MLLMs.
    *   **Well-Designed Dataset:** The DriveSOTIF dataset is a valuable contribution, providing a resource for training and evaluating models on safety-critical driving scenarios. The creation process using LLM-agents to generate data is also a strength, especially in handling corner cases and reducing human annotation cost.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough evaluation of the fine-tuned models, including qualitative analysis, quantitative metrics, and real-world testing. The analysis of the effect of quantization on real-time performance is also insightful.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors provide information on the model architectures, training procedures, and hyperparameters, increasing the reproducibility of the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Size:** The DriveSOTIF dataset, while valuable, has a relatively small size (1114 images). It needs to be substantially larger to cover a wider range of SOTIF scenarios and to fully leverage the potential of MLLMs. The paper acknowledges this limitation.
    *   **Complexity of the Driving Environment**:  While the results seem promising, the test scenarios may not adequately capture all levels of complexity and unpredictability associated with real-world environments.
